Katalog Artikel - Bagian 13 (50 Berita)

#5800. More Planned Riots Put Off Art, Fashion and Sport Events in Paris

Fashion and art institutions are preparing for another fraught weekend after hundreds of stores were looted in Paris, Milan and other European cities last weekend, as rioters took advantage of the disruption caused by protestors advocating for better cost of living and education conditions. It comes at a busy moment for the French capital in particular, which this weekend is set to play host to a series of events in the realms of art, sport and fashion — some of which has now been called off.

Art Basel Paris, which opened on Tuesday for its Avant-Première (super VIP) day, and on Wednesday, for the full VIP preview, canceled its Saturday and Sunday slots, now finishing at the end of the day on Friday. This decision was made “in light of potential demonstrations across Paris this weekend”, the fair organizers said in an email to press on Thursday, adding that it is “in the interest of safeguarding our visitors, exhibitors, partners, staff, and artworks”. It followed calls on social media for protestors to target the fair. Visitors attending the fair on Friday will be required to show government ID.

“This decision has not been taken lightly, and the safety of everyone involved in the fair remains our utmost priority,” Art Basel’s email to press continued. “Art Basel remains fully committed to serving galleries and artists, creating meaningful encounters with art and bringing our international community together.”

Other art museums and events, including Fondation Louis Vuitton, took a similar approach, and will also be closed for part or all of Saturday. Some events went ahead before anxieties about the protests heated up; on October 20, Miu Miu celebrated the premiere of Alice Dolop’s exhibition, Geste de Consolation, which was slated to run until October 25.

It’s not just the art scene facing disruption. This Sunday, the NFL’s Pittsburgh Steelers and the New Orleans Saints will play at the Stade de France. The NFL has said that it’s confident in its security teams and that the game will go on. Events surrounding the game, however, have been canceled, including a private brunch hosted by the NFL’s style editor.

Beauty brand Glow Recipe was set to host a pop-up on October 24 and 25, but announced on Instagram on Wednesday that the activation will not proceed as planned. “The safety of our community, our team, and everyone involved will always come first,” the brand posted. “To everyone who registered and was looking forward to joining us, thank you so much for your excitement, love, and understanding. We’re so grateful for this community and hope to share a little pink paradise with you in Paris very soon.”

The protests were a topic of conversation during this week’s luxury earnings calls, many of which had stores looted last weekend. Hermès executive vice president of finance Éric du Halgouët noted that the brand’s stores have since reopened in Paris, though its Marseille store remains closed.

“We’re first and foremost thinking about those who experienced these events firsthand, namely our employees and our customers, who were affected by acts of violence and looting,” he told investors on Thursday during the company’s earnings call. “The first measures we took were aimed at protecting our employees. We haven’t observed [any significant impact] over the past two days.”

“So this is another unfortunate thing happening,” Prada Group CEO Andrea Guerra said during the company’s earnings also on Thursday. “Luckily, I believe we were not touched by any kind of physical violence in our stores. We will obviously be open any other day and the next weekend. Obviously, if the authorities, the police, say we have to close, we will close. This is it: this is the world we live in today.”

Ernesto Greco, Ferragamo’s executive board member, told analysts that while its stores weren’t impacted in last Saturday’s riots, “We cannot exclude that if the disorders continue, the tourism, the local customer base could be tempted to either postpone or even to stop buying.”

Many stores that were looted last weekend have since reopened, including Printemps, Celine and Hermès. “We are pleased to announce the reopening of our store which had been closed following the destruction last weekend,” Celine CEO Séverine Merle posted to LinkedIn on Wednesday. “This reopening in record time was made possible thanks to the extraordinary mobilization of all the teams,” she wrote, thanking the store employees for their talent and passion.

LVMH hasn’t announced any French store closures ahead of the weekend for its brands, which include Louis Vuitton, Dior, Celine and Fendi.

During its earnings call on Thursday, Kering said five of its stores in France were looted, including four in Paris and one in Nice. The group added that it remains vigilant in the current context.

Those that do stay open risk further looting, though closing means losing another weekend of business after losing both sales and product less than a week ago. Risk is higher for small and independent boutiques, who don’t have the infrastructure and the inventory that the large luxury brands do.

Already, resale sites such as Vinted and Ebay are navigating what to do about stolen goods winding up on their platforms, and have confirmed they are increasing their monitoring of such items. A spokesperson for Vestiaire Collective says that the company’s curation, authentication, and fraud teams have immediately reinforced their monitoring processes. “We are actively tracking unusual behaviors and statistical anomalies that could be linked to recent events, with a particular focus on newly created seller profiles and abnormal listing patterns in specific categories. When doubts arise, targeted verification protocols are immediately triggered,” the spokesperson says.

Vestiaire Collective has held meetings with luxury brands this week to align on their course of action. “We have dedicated channels that allow victimized brands to share inventory alerts with our teams, enabling us to run targeted checks and immediately blacklist matching merchandise.

We also systematically comply with all official judicial requisitions to help authorities hold unlawful actors accountable,” the spokesperson adds.

Vestiaire is not ruling out the possibility that these heightened protocols will become the new normal, at least for the time being. “It is difficult to project an exact timeline for this ‘hyper vigilance’ phase,” Vestiaire’s spokesperson says. “However, our AI and monitoring models continuously learn from these heightened measures. If this trend of stolen items in the broader market continues, these reinforced protocols and stricter verification rules will simply become our new organizational baseline, ensuring that our ecosystem remains completely hostile to illicit activity.”

It’s too early to say whether the impact of the protests and looting will have an impact on brands due to reduced tourism to France, executives on Thursday’s calls agreed. “The question is whether recent events in France have an impact on tourism, but it is still too early to answer that question today,” Hermès’s du Halgouët said.

#5799. Tesla confirms Autopilot/FSD was ‘engaged’ when car crossed a median and killed a man

A Tesla crossed the grass median of Route 55 in New Jersey, hit a tree, slid into oncoming traffic, and struck an SUV head-on. The SUV’s driver, 68-year-old Phillip Eastman, was killed.

Police reported it as a driver who lost control. What they didn’t say, and likely didn’t know, is that Tesla’s own report to federal regulators says the car’s driver-assist system was engaged. This is part of an ongoing Electrek investigation matching reported crashes to Tesla’s redacted NHTSA data.

Around 8:31 a.m. on Saturday, August 22, 2026, a Tesla was heading south on Route 55 in Vineland, in Cumberland County, near milepost 34.6. The driver lost control, ran off the left side of the road, crossed the grass median, struck a tree, and continued into the northbound lanes, where the car hit a northbound Chevrolet SUV, according to the New Jersey State Police.

The SUV’s driver, Phillip “Phil” Eastman, 68, of Springfield, Pennsylvania, was killed. The Tesla’s driver and a passenger in the Chevrolet were hurt and taken to a hospital with moderate injuries.

Eastman had just been sworn in as a Springfield School District board member, and he spent nearly three decades coaching youth and high school ice hockey, including 17 years as the varsity head coach at Springfield High. His death was widely covered in the Philadelphia area.

And in all of it, no report mentioned Autopilot, “Full Self-Driving,” or any driver-assist system. The crash was told as a driver who lost control of his car.

Under a NHTSA Standing General Order, automakers must report crashes where a Level 2 driver-assist system was engaged within 30 seconds of impact and someone is killed. Tesla filed a report on this crash.

In it, Tesla identifies the car as a 2021 Model Y and marks the automation “Engagement Status” as “Verified Engaged” — meaning Tesla confirmed through its own telematics that Autopilot or “Full Self-Driving” was active. It lists the New Jersey State Police as the investigating agency, and says it learned of the crash from the media.

Then it redacts the fields that would explain what the car did: the crash narrative, the software version, and whether the road was within the system’s approved operating area. All blacked out as “confidential business information,” the same redactions Tesla applies to 99.9% of its reports.

We want to be precise about what this does and doesn’t prove. “Engaged within 30 seconds of impact” is the reporting trigger, not proof the system was steering when the car crossed the median. The driver may have been overriding it. And Tesla redacted the software version, so there’s no telling whether this was basic Autopilot or FSD.

Two more gaps are worth flagging. Tesla logs a pre-crash speed of just 37 mph, low for a freeway, which is more likely a single telematics snapshot from the pre-crash window than the speed at which the car left the road. And on this report Tesla checks only that it holds the car’s telematics, not the event-data recorder or video, so there may be less onboard evidence here than in other cases.

What is not in doubt is the one fact the public never got: a driver-assist system was on in a car that crossed a highway median and killed a man. Everything that would explain how is sealed.

This is the pattern the investigation keeps surfacing, and it’s why the redaction matters. Tesla is, more than ever, pushing owners to treat a Level 2 driver-assistance system as if it drives itself. It sells the software as “Full Self-Driving,” Elon Musk keeps promising unsupervised autonomy is close, and the branding invites drivers to trust the car.

But it is still Level 2. The human is driving and responsible at all times, and Tesla’s own actions confirm it: consumer FSD still requires supervision, and its robotaxi service is a small, closely watched fleet. At the moment Tesla is telling owners to trust the car, it hides the records that would show how the system behaves when things go wrong, and a man coaching kids’ hockey one season is a name in a sealed federal file the next.

How we matched it, and why it’s notable: Electrek flagged this crash weeks ago, from the news, as a fatal Tesla wreck that had no corresponding entry in the federal data, and no public mention of driver-assist. The latest NHTSA release added it, flagged as a fatality with the system verified engaged. The record is unredacted on location (Vineland), state, vehicle (2021 Model Y), the fatality, and the month, and the incident time Tesla logged, 12:29 UTC, converts to about 8:29 a.m. local, within two minutes of the 8:31 a.m. police reported. It’s the same crash. Electrek has reached out to the New Jersey State Police for the crash report and the vehicle data, and asked whether investigators received Tesla’s raw telemetry or only Tesla’s own interpretation of it.

"self-driving" car computers take data many times a second about what the vehicle is doing, what the software thinks is going on. All of that information should be preserved for crash investigators, the same way that it is in airplane crashes. And ultimately available to the public.

Strip the redaction away and this is a Tesla, running its driver-assist software, that crossed a median and killed a school board member in the oncoming lane. Leave it in place, as Tesla did, and it’s a driver who “lost control.” Those are very different stories, and the public got the one that leaves Tesla out of it.

We’re careful here. The system being on doesn’t prove it caused the crash, the driver may have been overriding it, and we don’t even know if it was Autopilot or FSD. That is exactly the problem. Tesla knows all of it. It has the telematics, it filed the report confirming the system was engaged, and it decided that what its car did in a fatal crash is a trade secret, down to which software was running. Phillip Eastman’s family, and the troopers still investigating, deserve the full account, not a redacted one.

Tesla is already under NHTSA investigation over how it reports these crashes. We’ll keep matching them, one by one, until the record is whole.

#5798. A Chef’s Guide to the Best Restaurants in Asheville, North Carolina

By Meherwan Irani as told to Jamie Feldmar

Part of what gives me so much joy about eating in Asheville is recognizing that we’re all part of a very integrated ecosystem. It feels like every restaurant I love is either run by an alum of one of the places I’ve been involved in, or by people that I’ve worked side by side with at hurricane-relief events, or by someone I know I’m going to see at the farmers market.

That’s what makes Asheville special. The folks that come in from out of town are lucky that there’s a virtuous cycle of talent here to keep the pipeline full. It’s so cool to see the evolution of this place, even if we locals don’t always realize how good we have it.It’s almost funny how not a big deal eating out here is. It’s so laid-back. We take it for granted that it’s going to be a great restaurant—of course it is! Having said that, the sheer range of restaurants is incredible. Given how small our town actually is, I sometimes find myself thinking, How lucky are we?

A hallmark of most of the restaurants I love is their connection to nearby farms. At my last count, there were more than a dozen farmers markets happening in Asheville, and there are probably more than twice as many local farms within an hour of here. Most people know the farmers by name, and nobody is precious about it. So when you go to a restaurant, they’ll just say, “It’s Evan’s greens tonight,” and everybody knows who Evan is.

The last thing is that whether it’s an Indian restaurant or a Filipino restaurant or Italian or Spanish, I love the Southern throughline in all of the dishes and hospitality here. That doesn’t mean there’s cornbread or okra in every dish; it’s honoring the fact that we all chose to open Indian or Italian restaurants here in Appalachia, so let’s not divorce the two from each other.

Let’s find a way to make it feel holistic. We do that really well. I was worried after the pandemic and Hurricane Helene that Asheville might not bounce back, but we have. I know that this place is going to continue to trailblaze for years to come.

20 Battery Park Avenue, Floor 1(828) 271-9808

Luminosa is hands down my wife, Molly, and my favorite date- night restaurant. It checks every box so beautifully. It’s intimate, but also a showstopper. It’s visually exciting to be in there, and yet [the design] doesn’t detract from the food, which they describe as “hyper-local Italian.” In the two years that they’ve been open, they’ve picked up a Michelin Green Star and a Michelin Bib Gourmand, and they deserve it. Everything about it just feels very intentional. The food is brilliantly delicious and the menu changes a lot—which sometimes drives me crazy, but it works here, because I know there’s going to be something fun and new every time I go. Their style of service and hospitality is so old-fashioned it almost borders on being antiquated, but I love it. It’s a small town at the end of the day, and everybody touches your table.

One word: respect. Katie Button and her crew have been at the top of their game for so long, you almost forget that continuing to win the championship is really hard to do. But they execute at the exact same incredibly high level as they did the first time I walked in years ago. I go there because what they do is incredible, and it’s always good to be reminded of how well they represent the tapas side of Spanish cuisine. It’s also a really exciting restaurant to be in—there’s an energy and a vibe because of the open kitchen, and it feels so intimate and fun. It’s always packed—they’re a victim of their own success—but even if I’m just going in for a glass of sherry and a plate of jamón ibérico, it’s worth pulling whatever favors I must to get in.

701 Haywood Road, Suite 102(828) 552-3880

This is a true only-in-Asheville pick, a chest-swelling-with-pride kind of choice. The chef, Silver Iocovozzi, worked with me at [two of Irani’s erstwhile restaurants] MG Road and Buxton Hall, and now they get to showcase their own cuisine, Filipino. Neng Jr.’s feels like the kind of restaurant that this city is built for. It’s so tiny and so intimate, with maybe 12 seats, so you’re packed in and you really feel like you’re eating dinner at Silver’s house. The food feels so personal—it’s brilliant and fun and totally them, with flavor profiles that remind me of Indian street food in some ways, full of fermentation and funk.

First of all, this space was converted into a restaurant without doing much to disguise the fact that it used to be a house. You’re dining on the front porch or in the side yard or in the living room, but somehow it manages to make you feel like you’re in a small village in Provence. The food has a French focus, as well as an Americana element. They win all kinds of awards for their wine program, deservedly so. Leo’s is hard to describe. It feels like this rustic little bistro where you might order something as simple as a grilled cheese sandwich and tomato soup, but when it shows up, you realize this could actually be the best grilled cheese and tomato soup you’ve ever had in your life. It’s totally unique and transportive, and it’s become my go-to spontaneous dinner recommendation for friends visiting from out of town.

I’m going to throw it back a little to one of the first places that really popped in West Asheville. Ten or 15 years ago, The Admiral was essentially a dive bar in a cinder-block building next to a gas station where probably 80 percent of Asheville’s pot deals went down. There, a young chef named Elliot Moss [ed note: later Irani’s partner at Buxton Hall] was putting out gastropub food that was blowing people’s minds. After service, they’d push all the tables to the side to clear room for these legendary dance parties. Now the neighborhood isn’t as sketchy, Elliot is long gone, and they no longer push the tables aside, but the food has not lost an inch. When you’re eating, you can just feel that it’s world-class cuisine, and reservations are hard to get, so it still feels special to get to go to The Admiral.

The best way I can describe chef Matt Dawes’s food, or at least my experience of it, is that it’s elevated British pub cuisine with a French influence and a Southern twist. The menu swings from steak frites au poivre to the best shepherd’s pie you’ve ever had to a pitch-perfect Welsh rarebit (it’s also the first place I had Welsh rabbit). The restaurant is located maybe 30 feet from the railroad tracks, next to a dirt field and one of the noisiest outdoor breweries in Asheville, but don’t let that deter you. It’s one of the most reliable and satisfying restaurants in the city.

This is a newcomer to the scene. Chef Ashleigh Shanti Santi, a James Beard Award–winner and prominent Top Chef contestant, has been a passionate advocate for Black Southern foodways. Good Hot Fish is exactly what it sounds like: a fish counter with amazing hot fish sandwiches and seafood, plus the perfect sides. All of the fish she serves comes from local waterways. There’s fried catfish and Gulf shrimp burgers and fish dip with fried saltines. It’s literally just a counter with a few stools against the wall, but the technique is immaculate. The sandwiches are served on squishy white bread with buttermilk tartare sauce, and it delivers on its promise of being the best damn hot fish sandwich you’ve ever had.

I have to give a shout-out here to one of the OGs. To call All Souls just great pizza does it a disservice; you need to understand the backstory. David Bauer, was one of the first bread gurus/savants of Asheville. Farm and Sparrow, his bakery, helped usher in the modern renaissance of heirloom grains and bread that you now see everywhere. He grew his own grain, milled his own flour, and made these amazing breads. Then he helped open All Souls, which makes pizza using stone-ground heirloom grains, in a style I can only describe as Carolina pizza. It’s our own thing, with our own grains, cooked in a wood-fired oven with toppings that come from within 50 miles or so. It’s in a building that was a diner for decades in West Asheville, next to a big old field where you can spread out a picnic blanket if the restaurant is full. All Souls is a classic example of doing one thing, doing it well, and not being too uptight about it, which is everything I love about Asheville.

244 Short Coxe Avenue, #110(828) 505-1003

I have to include two bakeries here. Mother serves a full lunch and dinner menu, so it’s a little more on the restaurant side of things. It’s again in a weird part of Asheville, sandwiched between a sauna and a tire shop, with garage doors that open to a front patio that looks like a loading ramp for trucks. And yet, like so many places here, it’s totally transportive. You feel like you’re eating in Europe somehow. Their jambon-beurre sandwich is the closest I’ve had to Fauchon’s in Paris, and their pumpernickel bread is divine. It feels like a restaurant run by a group of passionate friends that care deeply about food and hospitality, who all got together to open a little place as a gift to the community, because that’s exactly what it is.

I would describe Owl as a bakery with Southern sensibilities and French technique. I’d put the lamination on their croissants up against anything in Stockholm. They’re known for their almond rose croissants, cardamom buns, and squirrel bread [a seed-and-nut loaf], and if you don’t get there right at 8 a.m. they’re going to be sold out. Sometimes I look at the people lining up, and I want to shake each and every one of them and yell, “Do you have any idea how good you have it right now?!” I actually have to resist doing that as I see them gabbing away over their morning pastries and lattes without a care in the world, not recognizing that the rest of us have to schlep around the world God knows how many times to find anything even close to this good in terms of quality and execution. And that’s just life in Asheville.

It’s an honorable mention, because I haven’t stepped foot in the restaurant for 17 years, but I get takeout from Rocky’s almost every Sunday, when I host a men’s group at my house where we discuss things like “How do you actually define electro music?” and play Crocodile, a weird Canadian board game. In the very crowded field of hot fried chicken, Rocky’s stands at the top, because their chicken is actually melt-your-face hot, not “oh-look-how-cute” Nashville hot. Beginners should get the medium. The hot is for veterans, and the extra hot is something the CIA should use to extract information. The crust is thin and it has the perfect amount of crunch, and the seasoning goes all the way through. But most importantly, every week we get the same order, and never once in 17 years have they messed up that order. I feel like a highly underrated aspect of a great restaurant is doing exactly the same thing 52 weeks out of the year, and I have mad respect for Rocky’s.

Recommendations on where to dine, shop, and stay, wherever you’re headed next.

Meherwan Irani, of Chai Pani and Spicewalla, shares his go-to places to eat and drink in his adopted hometown.

Then he helped open All Souls, which makes pizza using stone-ground heirloom grains, in a style I can only describe as Carolina pizza. It’s our own thing, with our own grains, cooked in a wood-fired oven with toppings that come from within 50 miles or so. It’s in a building that was a diner for decades in West Asheville, next to a big old field where you can spread out a picnic blanket if the restaurant is full. All Souls is a classic example of doing one thing, doing it well, and not being too uptight about it, which is everything I love about Asheville.

244 Short Coxe Avenue, #110(828) 505-1003

I have to include two bakeries here. Mother serves a full lunch and dinner menu, so it’s a little more on the restaurant side of things. It’s again in a weird part of Asheville, sandwiched between a sauna and a tire shop, with garage doors that open to a front patio that looks like a loading ramp for trucks. And yet, like so many places here, it’s totally transportive. You feel like you’re eating in Europe somehow. Their jambon-beurre sandwich is the closest I’ve had to Fauchon’s in Paris, and their pumpernickel bread is divine. It feels like a restaurant run by a group of passionate friends that care deeply about food and hospitality, who all got together to open a little place as a gift to the community, because that’s exactly what it is.

I would describe Owl as a bakery with Southern sensibilities and French technique. I’d put the lamination on their croissants up against anything in Stockholm. They’re known for their almond rose croissants, cardamom buns, and squirrel bread [a seed-and-nut loaf], and if you don’t get there right at 8 a.m. they’re going to be sold out. Sometimes I look at the people lining up, and I want to shake each and every one of them and yell, “Do you have any idea how good you have it right now?!” I actually have to resist doing that as I see them gabbing away over their morning pastries and lattes without a care in the world, not recognizing that the rest of us have to schlep around the world God knows how many times to find anything even close to this good in terms of quality and execution. And that’s just life in Asheville.

It’s an honorable mention, because I haven’t stepped foot in the restaurant for 17 years, but I get takeout from Rocky’s almost every Sunday, when I host a men’s group at my house where we discuss things like “How do you actually define electro music?” and play Crocodile, a weird Canadian board game. In the very crowded field of hot fried chicken, Rocky’s stands at the top, because their chicken is actually melt-your-face hot, not “oh-look-how-cute” Nashville hot. Beginners should get the medium. The hot is for veterans, and the extra hot is something the CIA should use to extract information. The crust is thin and it has the perfect amount of crunch, and the seasoning goes all the way through. But most importantly, every week we get the same order, and never once in 17 years have they messed up that order. I feel like a highly underrated aspect of a great restaurant is doing exactly the same thing 52 weeks out of the year, and I have mad respect for Rocky’s.

Recommendations on where to dine, shop, and stay, wherever you’re headed next.

#5797. 'A uniquely direct route that might cause damage': Scientists uncover link between air pollution and eye diseases like glaucoma

A review of 41 studies found that higher exposure to air pollution was linked to greater odds of glaucoma and cataracts, with PM2.5 pollution showing the clearest association.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Aging is one of the biggest risk factors for developing glaucoma or cataracts — but as scientists look more closely at what happens to the eyes over a lifetime, they are finding that people's exposure to air pollution also plays a role in whether they develop these eye conditions.

Glaucoma, which affects about 80 million people worldwide, is a group of diseases that damage the optic nerve, which carries visual information from the eye to the brain. It can lead to permanent vision loss, usually affecting peripheral vision at first and later central vision. Cataracts are even more common, affecting more than 94 million people globally; the condition occurs when the eye's lens becomes cloudy, causing blurred vision and, eventually, blindness.

Aging raises the risk of both diseases, as do certain medical conditions, such as diabetes. But now, a review of 41 studies suggests that people who are exposed to higher levels of air pollution may also face a greater risk of these diseases. The research, presented in September at the 44th Congress of the European Society of Cataract and Refractive Surgeons in London, brought together evidence from dozens of studies collectively involving millions of people across East Asia and the U.K.

The studies were observational, meaning they can only draw a link between air pollution and the eye conditions, rather than prove that air pollution causes the diseases. But still, "this reframes glaucoma and cataract as conditions shaped by where you live and breathe, not just how long you've lived," said study co-author Dr. Ahmed Alnabihi, an ophthalmologist at King Khaled Eye Specialist Hospital and Research Center in Saudi Arabia, who presented the findings. These environmental exposures may compound age-related decline in the eye as they do in cardiovascular disease, he said.

The researchers examined exposure to several pollutants, including different sizes of particulate matter (PM), such as fine PM2.5 and larger PM10. They also looked at nitrogen dioxide, sulfur dioxide, ozone and carbon monoxide.

Most studies included in the review estimated people's long-term exposure based on the pollution levels around their homes and in their communities. They used a range of methods, including government monitoring systems, satellite data and computer models designed to estimate pollution levels. A few studies examined short-term exposure and its relation to more sudden eye issues, such as acute angle-closure glaucoma, a medical emergency that can quickly cause vision loss.

Using this wealth of data, the team compared pollution exposure with rates of glaucoma, cataracts and age-related macular degeneration (AMD), another common eye condition that impairs vision. PM2.5 — tiny particles that measure 2.5 micrometers or smaller — showed the clearest associations with glaucoma and cataract risk.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

People with the highest exposure to PM2.5 had 70% higher odds of glaucoma, compared to those with the least exposure. Plus, an increase in PM2.5 exposure equivalent to the difference between the 25th and 75th percentiles of exposure within each study was associated with 8% higher odds of glaucoma and a 4% higher hazard of cataracts.

Smoke from burning agricultural residue contains fine particulate matter. In a review of 41 studies, higher PM2.5 exposure showed a strong association with glaucoma and cataract.

The evidence was less consistent for air pollution and age-related macular degeneration, which damages the central part of the retina that's responsible for sharp, detailed vision. And unlike the clear connection seen with PM2.5, other pollutants showed less consistent links to eye disease. For instance, nitrogen dioxide was not significantly tied to AMD, ozone was not tied to cataracts, and neither pollutant was linked to glaucoma. Sulfur dioxide and PM10 showed no significant associations with any of the three conditions.

The association between PM2.5 and glaucoma was relatively consistent across the reviewed studies, said Nidhi Singh, an environmental epidemiologist at the IUF-Leibniz Research Institute for Environmental Medicine in Germany, who was not involved in the study. The findings for PM2.5 and cataracts were moderately consistent, albeit more variable than the glaucoma data; still, she said that the analysis seems to support a link.

Singh did caution that the underlying evidence is observational. "Therefore, the results demonstrate an association, not by themselves a causal effect of PM2.5 on glaucoma or cataract," she told Live Science.

The exact biological mechanisms behind these links remain unclear, but Alnabihi noted that fine particulate matter is small enough to be absorbed into the body. This can trigger oxidative stress, in which harmful molecules overwhelm natural defenses and cause damage. It also drives chronic, low-grade inflammation, in which the immune system stays mildly activated for long periods, he explained.

In the eye's lens, these processes can "damage the crystallin proteins that keep it transparent, causing them to misfold and cloud, which is what a cataract is," Alnabihi said.

In the optic nerve, the same processes are thought to damage retinal ganglion cells, a special type of neuron that relays information from the retina to the brain. This can contribute to glaucoma independent of internal eye pressure, he said, which is another big risk factor.

"Unlike the lungs or gut, its [the eye's] surface is in direct, continuous contact with outdoor air, giving pollutants a uniquely direct route that might cause damage," he added.

Other studies have found clues that support the link between PM2.5 and eye damage. Mice that were exposed to ambient PM2.5 for nine months developed elevated pressure inside their eyes, a glaucoma risk factor. The researchers observed both oxidative stress and nitrative stress — a similar phenomenon — in the tissues that drain fluid from the eye, which may help to explain the rise in pressure.

Studies of humans and human cells have uncovered similar patterns. In data from more than 480,000 people in the UK Biobank, a large repository of health information, those exposed to higher PM2.5 were more likely to develop glaucoma over time. In another study, scientists exposed lab-grown human retinal tissue made from stem cells to PM2.5. The particles disrupted retinal cell development, including that of retinal ganglion cells, while increasing cell death.

These studies offer pieces of a possible mechanism, but for now, how those pieces fit together in people remains unclear.

This article is for informational purposes only and is not meant to offer medical advice.

Sanket Jain is an independent journalist and documentary photographer based in Western India’s Maharashtra state. Sanket’s work has been featured in over 35 publications, including MIT Technology Review, Devex, Wired, Telegraph, Thomson Reuters Foundation, The Nation, British Medical Journal, Verge, USA Today, Progressive Magazine and others. He was the winner of the 2025 Eric and Wendy Schmidt Award for Excellence in Science Communications. He was awarded an honorable mention for the 2026 Evert Clark/Seth Payne Award, which recognized a Live Science article and three others that addressed the impacts of air pollution and extreme heat on physical and mental health.

Expect full-size tools, a battery voucher, and fun extras, plus a storage case for all the loot.

Craftsman is getting in on the Advent-calendar game this holiday season. The iconic red-and-black tool brand’s first limited edition box set is back in stock and bound to sell out fast. All in all, it amounts to $200 worth of stuff—including a battery voucher—reasonably priced at just $65.

#5795. Copycat In-N-Out Burger

A copycat In-N-Out burger, served "Animal Style," isn't just a burger. It's a way of life.

When I get back to Los Angeles, my first stop is at In-N-Out Burger for an Animal Style Double-Double. I live in Chicago now, but when I worked in L.A. restaurants, In-N-Out was always the reward waiting on the other side of a closing shift. This copycat version brings that exact order to my midwestern kitchen, from the mustard-fried patty to the precise way everything gets stacked.

“Animal Style” in In-N-Out parlance means the patties get fried with a smear of mustard (which gives it a tangy, caramelized crust you can’t get any other way), covered in cheese, and served with a heap of caramelized onions. Most important is the spread. I am so obsessed with copycat In-N-Out sauce that I’ll put it on just about anything. Like most Double-Doubles, this one has crisp iceberg lettuce, tomato slices, dill pickle chips and a soft, buttered, grilled bun that holds everything together.

Every bite of this burger hits salty, sweet and tangy all at once. That combination is exactly why I still crave this burger more than almost anything else in Southern California. Pair this burger with a batch of Animal Style fries, and you’ve got the whole drive-thru experience (minus the incessantly long lines) without ever leaving your kitchen.

Combine the mayonnaise, ketchup, sweet pickle relish, white vinegar and sugar in a small bowl. Cover and refrigerate the sauce for at least one hour.

Editor’s Tip: Make a double batch of this sauce. You’ll want extra for dipping fries.

In a large cast-iron skillet, heat the oil over medium heat. Add the onions and the remaining salt. Cook and stir the onions until they’re softened. Reduce the heat to medium-low and cook them for 30 to 35 minutes or until they’re deep golden brown, stirring occasionally. Remove them from the skillet and keep them warm.

Editor’s Tip: If you’re short on time, try caramelizing onions in the microwave. It works!

Spread 2 tablespoons of softened butter over the cut sides of the buns. Toast the buns in the skillet over medium-high heat, cut sides down. Remove them to serving plates, and wipe the skillet clean.

Shape the ground beef into four 2-ounce, 1/2-inch-thick patties. Sprinkle them with salt and pepper. Add the remaining 2 tablespoons of butter to the same skillet. Cook the burgers, covered, over medium heat until a thermometer reads 160°F, four to five minutes on each side. While cooking, brush them with mustard. Top each patty with a slice of cheese. Continue cooking the patties until the cheese melts.

Editor’s Tip: To get a nice mustardy caramelization, add the mustard to the patty before flipping it, then swipe a little more on the top of the burger before adding the cheese.

Spread the chilled sauce over the bottom half of each bun, then layer on the lettuce, tomato slices and dill pickle chips. Place one patty on top, add a generous spoonful of caramelized onions, then stack on the second patty and finish the burgers with the bun top.

These burgers are best eaten fresh out of the skillet with the buns still warm and the patties hot. That said, you can store the components separately in airtight containers in the refrigerator for up to two days. Keep the sauce, caramelized onions, cooked patties and fresh toppings apart so nothing gets soggy, then reheat the patties in a skillet before reassembling. The sauce alone will keep in the fridge for about five days.

No In-N-Out-inspired meal feels complete without a side of Animal Style fries, smothered in the same combo of spread, caramelized onions and melted cheese. A crisp, cold milkshake (make mine malted) is another classic pairing if you’re going all in on the fast-food nostalgia. For something a little lighter, a simple green salad or a pile of dill pickle chips on the side keeps things balanced.

Animal Style is part of In-N-Out’s “Not So Secret Menu” that turns a regular burger into something a little more indulgent. The patty gets cooked with mustard on it, then the burger is loaded up with extra spread and a hearty helping of grilled onions. This copycat recipe builds that method right into the directions, so you get all that flavor without needing to know any insider lingo.

To make this recipe ahead of time, start by making the sauce and caramelizing the onions a day or two in advance. Store both in the refrigerator, then cook the patties and toast the buns fresh right before serving so everything tastes its best.

#5793. This New G-Shock Looks a Lot Like One of the Most Iconic Watches Ever Made

If we were playing matchmaker for some of our favorite watch brands, we wouldn’t have thought of pairing a Casio G-Shock with Patek Philippe’s iconic Nautilus. However, the Japanese watchmaker’s new GMC-2500 proves that opposites do attract.

The 2500 Series is a new collection that “reimagines the design DNA of the watch that started it all,” according to Casio. The brand is nodding towards the original G-Shock ref. DW-5000C from 1983, a watch designed to be tough enough to keep ticking even when dropped. The team famously tested prototypes by hurling them out of the third-floor men’s bathroom of the Casio offices. The new 2500 series is a close descendant of the shock-absorbent watch's original design.

The first G-Shock featured an octagonal digital display, four buttons on the case flanks, and an integrated rubber strap. The 2500 Series incorporates most of those features—but this time, the display is analog rather than digital, and a pair of case “ears” makes the model look a lot like Patek Philippe’s famous flagship luxury sports watch.

Designed by Gérald Genta and released in 1976, the Nautilus is based on a porthole—hence the “ears,” which mirror the hinges/securing mechanism used to open and close a ship’s window against the elements. You may also recall that this isn't the first time that Casio has taken inspiration from a famous Genta watch: the GA-2100 Series (aka ‘CasiOak’), with its octagonal bezel design, bears a remarkable similarity to the Audemars Piguet Royal Oak.

The watch community has been waiting impatiently for the announcement of the 2500 series since Casio’s Japanese website accidentally listed these pieces back in September. In the months since, collectors have already given the watch the “CasioNaut” nickname. (What good is a watch without a clever nickname, after all?) The wait isn’t over just yet, though. The line will officially go on sale through Casio’s website on November 6th.

The 2500 Series is much more than a quartz-powered Nautilus copy. At the center of the design is the Multi-Mission Date function that operates through a three-cell display at 3 o’clock. Besides indicating the date, it can also display the watch’s current mode, elapsed stopwatch minutes, and setting information. Meanwhile, a small-seconds display at 9 o’clock shows passing seconds, but can also be paused and restarted as necessary. (Pausing the function can extend the battery life up to five years.)

The timepiece's tech uses G-SHOCK’s familiar four-button arrangement and comes packed with all the features we've come to expect from the Japanese brand. For starters, the watch automatically keeps rigorous time through 2099 after you input the year, month, and date. LED illumination ensures everything is legible even in low light, while a magnetic system steadies the watch’s hands even if dropped, bumped, or roughed up. Water-resistant to 200m, the 2500 Series is just as capable as you’d expect from a hard-wearing G-SHOCK. Available in simple resin cases with matching rubber bracelets for $145; with metal accents and rubber bracelets for $280-$300; and with full-metal bracelets for $350, the handful of launch references come in a variety of versatile colors and weigh between 47 grams (resin) and 160-180 grams (metal).

Though Casio releases oodles of fresh colorways in existing G-SHOCK families each year, it’s not every day that the world gets an entirely new model family. Fun, streamlined, and much more affordable than the watch it's nicknamed after, the 2500 Series is certainly on its way to becoming a hit.

#5792. How to Make Better Pie Crust, According to a Pastry Chef

There is a lot of subtlety that goes into making a good pie crust, the types of intuitive moves or small tips you pick up in a professional kitchen (or by baking with an experienced relative). But a few principles make all the difference: keeping the butter cold, avoiding overmixing, letting the dough rest, and baking the finished pie long enough.

Below, and in my book A Good Bake, I cover the little things that matter, from making and rolling out pie dough to crimping the crust and knowing when a pie is done. These are the techniques I use to make delicious, professional-looking pies, no matter what recipe I’m following.

A Good Bake by Melissa Weller with Carolyn Carreno

In a professional bakery, cooks prepare pie dough up to several days in advance. Set yourself up the same way at home. Making the dough ahead gives it time to rest and chill, so it’s ready to roll when you need it. You can also freeze pie dough up to one month in advance; wrap it well and defrost it overnight in the fridge.

The essential ingredients in pie dough are flour, water, a pinch of salt, maybe a very small amount of sugar, and some fat. Many traditional American pie dough recipes call for lard, rendered pork fat, which makes for an exceptionally flaky crust.

I love lard, but quality lard is not always easy to find, so I use good old-fashioned unsalted butter for my pie and tart crusts. With good technique, you can achieve plenty of flakiness and get that great buttery flavor.

Some big pieces, some smaller pieces, just right.

Many pie recipes call for cutting the butter into ¼" cubes. I prefer largish ½" chunks. After cutting the butter into pieces, I arrange them onto a plate and freeze them for 10 minutes so the butter gets as cold as can be without turning rock solid.

When mixed into the flour, some larger chunks remain intact. When you roll out the dough, those chunks form striations of butter. As the crust bakes, the water in the butter turns to steam, creating microscopic flaky layers—and a light, tender crust.

Looking for maximum flake? Check out our guide to laminating pie dough →

Lots of recipes call for adding ice water to the dough. I find that cold water from my refrigerator (like from a water filter or door dispenser) works fine. That said, if you want to use super-chilled water, it certainly won’t hurt.

Add water only after you mix the flour and butter. When the water joins the party, gluten starts to form. Too much gluten development toughens pie dough, making the finished crust heavy and dense rather than light and flaky. Mixing the flour with cold butter first coats the flour’s proteins, impeding the interaction between the water and the flour and reducing gluten development.

It’s okay if there are still some floury bits!

After bringing the dough together, I shape it into a round disk or brick, depending on the shape I’ll roll it into. Then I wrap it in plastic wrap, run a rolling pin over it to compact and flatten it, and put it in the refrigerator to rest.

A good rest lets the flour fully absorb any moisture so that the dough comes together completely. Resting the dough also relaxes the gluten, making it easier to roll out and less likely to shrink in the oven.

If your kitchen runs warm, work quickly and give the dough extra time in the refrigerator whenever it starts to feel soft or tacky. You can also chill your mixing bowl, bench scraper, and rolling pin before you start.

Take the dough out of the refrigerator, put it on the counter, and let it sit until it feels malleable but still firm (10 to 15 minutes) before rolling. The goal is for the dough to be just pliable enough to roll but not so soft that it starts to stick. Conversely, rolling our cold dough straight from the fridge can cause it to crack.

Once you start rolling, move quickly. Even the firmest dough block will soften quickly under the friction of the rolling pin and the heat of your hands. The whole process shouldn’t take more than a minute or two.

It may be unconventional, but I always butter a pie plate before arranging the dough. The butter helps hold the dough in place as I shape it and provides insurance for removing it from the pan after baking. I smear enough butter on the bottom and sloped sides of the plate to leave a visible layer. I usually use cold butter, but slightly softened also works.

There are two ways to “line” a pie plate—that is, to make sure the dough fits snugly inside. Either fold the dough in half, lift it from the surface you rolled it out on, and place it in the pan. Or roll the dough onto your rolling pin and carry it over on the pin. I fold the dough when lining a round pie plate, and I roll it onto a rolling pin when using a jelly roll pan, which requires a larger piece. Use whatever method you find easiest.

In either case, carry the dough until it hovers over the pie plate, tart pan, jelly roll pan, or other vessel, centering it as much as you can. Then gently lay it onto the dish and unfold or unroll it. Adjust the dough so that it’s centered on the pan.

Ease the pie dough into the bottom corners of the pan without stretching it.

Gently lift one edge of the dough with one hand and use the other to press it into the bottom of the pan, working your way around the perimeter. Use a delicate touch, and resist pulling the dough, which will cause it to spring back when baked and create thin spots in the crust.

Next, gently run your finger along the rim to press the dough onto it, just enough to ensure it adheres. This allows you to see how much dough is overhanging. Make sure it is even all the way around and sitting nicely on top of the rim.

Use kitchen shears to trim the overhanging dough so there is an even 1" hanging over the perimeter of the pie dish. (Discard the trimmings or smush them together into a disk and refrigerate or freeze the dough to make a pie another day.)

If you are making a double-crust or lattice-topped pie, trim the top sheet of dough or lattice so it is flush with the outside edge of the pan.

Keep it even: Place your thumb in the indent from your index finger just made.

In pie making, “crimping” means forming the decorative, wavy edge around the perimeter of a pie. To crimp, tightly roll the overhanging dough toward the center of the pie to form a thick lip that rests on the ridge of the plate. While many recipes call for folding the excess dough under, I prefer folding it up and over. When you’re finished, the dough shouldn't hang over the rim.

The process is the same whether you are making a single- or double-crust pie. For a double crust, roll the bottom crust over the top crust, sealing the filling inside.

As you form this ridge, use your fingers to shape the edge of the rolled crust so that it is rounded and even. Once the entire edge is rolled, place the index finger and thumb of one hand on the inside edge of the ridge and use the index finger of your other hand to push the outside edge toward the center of the pie, forming a V or U shape. Continue around the perimeter until you have crimped the entire crust.

There is a lot of chilling when making pie. First, you chill the butter. Then you chill the finished dough before rolling it out. You’ll also want to chill the pie dish after lining it, and again after crimping and filling the dough, before baking.

If you’ve ever wondered why pies in professional bakeries look so much prettier than your homemade ones, it’s because they chill them—or even freeze them—before baking. Chilling the uncooked pie helps it hold its shape better during baking.

In my kitchens, we often freeze a fully assembled fruit pie (overnight is best), then bake it directly from frozen. To bake a frozen pie, put it in the oven straight from the freezer and add 10 to 20 minutes to the total baking time, using the recipe’s visual indicators for doneness in the recipe.

Always put a baking sheet under pies before baking. This is especially important with fruit pies, to prevent the bubbling fruit juices from spilling over the edge of the pan onto your oven floor.

Also called pre- or par-baking, blind baking refers to baking the crust before adding the filling. For many custard pies, which cook faster than fruit pies, you’ll partially blind-bake the crust to keep it from getting soggy or even staying raw. For pies and tarts whose fillings are not baked at all, you’ll blind-bake the crust until it’s fully golden brown

To blind-bake a crust, line the shaped, crimped, unbaked pie shell with parchment paper (crinkle the paper first to fit the pie shell better), then fill the parchment-lined dough with pie weights to prevent the crust from puffing while it bakes. BA’s favorite pie weights are inexpensive steel ball bearings, but many cooks use dried beans or rice; label any food items you use as “pie weights” when the pie is done, since they will not be good for cooking after baking.

For the full technique, see BA’s guide to blind-baking pie crust.

Depending on how cold the pie is when you put it in the oven—and depending on your oven’s hot spots and other quirks—baking time can vary, so I always rely on the recipe’s visual cues for doneness.

The crust should be a rich shade of golden brown. For a fruit pie, the filling must be bubbling through the lattice in the center of the pie or the vent cut into its top. If the filling isn’t bubbling in the center (not just around the sides), the fruit hasn’t cooked down enough. Remove it too early, and you risk a filling that weeps when you cut a slice.

Custard pies must bake until they are set. A set custard will wobble slightly when you jiggle the pan, but it shouldn’t slosh around like liquid. Avoid baking a custard pie so long that it’s fully set, which can cause surface cracks as the pie cools.

Cooling time is essential to pie making. Custard pies need to set (and sometimes chill for several hours), and fruit pies need ample time to gel and firm. For beautiful slices that hold their shape, the wait is worthwhile.

Overmixing or overhandling the dough encourages gluten development, which can make the baked crust tough rather than tender. Mix only until the dough begins to come together; it should still look a little shaggy, with visible pieces of butter.

Pie dough can shrink if it hasn’t rested long enough or if you stretch it while fitting it into the pan. Let the dough rest in the refrigerator before rolling, gently ease it into the pie plate without pulling, and chill the shaped crust again before baking.

Slumped crusts can happen if you remove the pie weights too early or you don’t fill the crust enough. Add weights to fill the crust to the rim of the pie dish, and don’t remove them until the crust is set (it should look dry and the parchment will no longer stick).

The butter may have gotten too warm or worked too thoroughly into the flour. For distinct flaky layers, keep the butter cold and leave some larger pieces intact. If the dough becomes soft or tacky while you're working, return it to the refrigerator until firm.

Why does my pie dough crack when I roll it out?

Dough that’s too cold can crack when you roll it, while dough that hasn’t hydrated enough may crumble. Let chilled dough sit until it’s pliable but still firm before rolling. If it remains dry and won't hold together, the problem may be insufficient hydration.

Editor’s note: Originally published in November 2020, this guide has been updated with additional troubleshooting advice by Phoebe Fry.

#5789. 5 Cookbooks That Go Deep on a Single Ingredient

If you’ve ever been starstruck by a perfect Cinderella pumpkin or a stately stalk of Brussels sprouts, you likely understand the appeal of a book focused on a single fruit or vegetable. While books like this aren’t always long, they typically go deep, with the fervor of an author who is fully obsessed.

One charming example, The Core of an Onion: Peeling the Rarest Common Food by Mark Kurlansky, melds authority with a dose of irreverence. First published in 2023, it’s a slim 200 pages, but the writer (who’s also written nonfiction books dedicated to oysters, salt, and cod) packs a lot in. “Onions have a way of taking on unexpected significance,” he posits. The first section covers the allium’s history and cultural impact. (Did you know that ancient Egyptians tied them to mummies’ feet as a preservative?) Kurlansky then offers loosely structured recipes for breads, sauces, and more, all centering the humble bulb.

Elevating a perhaps overlooked ingredient is a core tenet of single-­subject cookbookery. In The Compleat Squash: A Passionate Grower’s Guide to Pumpkins, Squash, and Gourds, a canonical 2004 ode to members of the Cucurbitaceae ­family, master ­gardener Amy Goldman Fowler’s passion is abundantly evident. “Sometimes I don’t know where pumpkins begin and I end,” she writes, before segueing into a series of squash and gourd portraits, each showing an individual specimen more striking than the last. Fowler shares recipes too, in addition to breed spotlights, resources for seed ­buying, and advice on growing your own.

Some such tomes aim to introduce the reader to the joys of less ubiquitous ingredients. Lane Selman and Margarett Waterbury’s Radicchio: A Celebration of the Beloved Italian Vegetable tells the “human and natural history” of the bitter chicory, along with instructions for how to grow it and cook with it. Selman is the founder of the Culinary Breeding Network, an initiative that rallies scientists, farmers, and chefs to geek out over new produce varieties. The book gets weird in the best way possible, with profiles of Italian growers and recipes including gnocchi with radicchio pesto, radicchio with prosciutto ­gratin, and chocolate–radicchio cake (why not?).

Radicchio reads almost like a community cookbook, and that’s intentional. Selman’s inspiration to pursue the topic was the sheer lack of writing on this oddball vegetable: “All of these people that we’ve met on our way down the rabbit hole needed to be included,” she says. “They’re the ones who have given us all this information. Sometimes it takes a village.”

Other single-subject cookbooks showcase pantry ­staples that are rarely in the spotlight. Anna Hezel’s Little Book of Olives: A Guide to Cooking, Eating, and Drinking for the Brine-Inclined presents recipes including cantaloupe and crushed-olive panzanella, Castelvetrano and pistachio pesto, and a whole chapter of olive desserts, plus shopping tips and a breakdown of varieties. The book’s retro aesthetic lends ­gravitas to an ingredient often relegated to garnish status.

Finally, some books exist more to dazzle than to inform, as is the case with pop artist Ed Ruscha and River Café chef Ruthie Rogers’s 2025 release Squeeze Me: Lemon Recipes & Art. In the pair’s high-gloss love letter to the lemon, pop art–coded photos with cheeky captions and a suite of minimalist recipes—such as lemon–­mascarpone tart and limoncello—make this ode to the simple citrus equally at home on a coffee table or the kitchen shelf.

While styles and forms vary, these books serve a ­common goal: to illuminate the sometimes surprising pleasures of a particular fruit or vegetable. That they manage to do so in such delightful fashion is proof that, once in a while, ­working with less really is more.

Hungry for even more stories to sink your teeth into?

These laser-focused titles geek out on radicchio, lemons, olives, and more.

#5788. BMW’s new i3 just drove 753 miles on a single charge

BMW put the new i3 to the test to showcase the power of its Gen6 batteries. The long-wheelbase i3 covered 753 miles (1,213 km) without stopping, the longest covered by any BMW EV to date.

BMW Group began series production of its sixth-generation (Gen6) EV batteries in Shenyang on October 15, its third global production site for the new battery tech.

To prove the power of its latest battery tech, set to power its Neue Klasse lineup, BMW put the new i3 50L through an endurance test to see how far it can really drive.

BMW said the new cylindrical cells offer over 20% higher energy density, enabling 30% faster charging and longer driving range than the previous generation.

After unveiling the i3 50L, a long-wheelbase version developed specifically for China at the Chengdu Auto Show in August, BMW said it would deliver a CLTC range of over 621 miles (1,000 km), topping the iX3 50L with a CLTC range of 571 miles (919 km).

During the event, the i3 50L managed over 753 miles (1,213 km) on a single charge, a new record for a BMW electric vehicle, the company said. The electric sedan achieved an extremely efficient 8.8 kWh per 100 km, covering highways, city and rural streets.

While BMW said it was a brand record, not a global one, it was enough to top the Mercedes Vision EQXX, which traveled 1,202 km from Germany to the UK in 2022.

In June, BMW drove an iX3 50L prototype over 500 miles (800 km) around the Qinghai Lake grand loop. It achieved an energy consumption of 12.6 kWh per 100 km.

The long-wheelbase i3 uses a 113.4 kWh battery that delivers a CLTC range of over 621 miles (1,000 km). Thanks to its 800V architecture, the i3 50L can add 248 miles (400 km) of range in 10 minutes with up to 400 kW fast charging.

BMW opened orders for the iX3 50L at the Chengdu Auto Show in August, starting at RMB 269,900 ($40,000). The i3 50L is expected to go on sale later this year, with customer deliveries set to begin in the first quarter of 2027.

I think I need a bio break long before the battery gives up on long trips. The strength of these is actually the apartment owners that only occasionally recharge. Much easier, almost like an ICE.

In the US, the 2027 BMW iX3 50 xDrive is now available to order, starting at $61,500. It has an EPA-estimated driving range of up to 434 miles.

The 2027 BMW i3 is also on sale starting at the same price of $61,500. The electric sedan delivers up to 468 miles of range.

#5787. Rising temperatures could boost pregnancy risks worldwide — here's how

As climate change fuels extreme temperatures and makes severely hot days more frequent, doctors are concerned about the health impacts on pregnant people.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Rising temperatures around the globe are contributing to a range of health risks, from infectious disease to food insecurity and wildfire smoke. Extreme heat is another major public health concern, as climate change drives up both average temperatures and the frequency of extremely hot days. This August was the world's hottest on record, and it drove upticks in heat-related emergency visits and deaths in various U.S. states.

Hotter weather is expected to pose dangerous health risks for millions, and pregnant people are especially vulnerable to its impacts, experts caution.

"Pregnant people are physiologically more vulnerable to heat," Dr. Susanna Esposito, a pediatrician at the University of Parma in Italy, told Live Science. "Climate adaptation should treat pregnant people as a priority population."

Extreme heat is linked to adverse birth outcomes that can be fatal for both mothers and newborns. Heat can also hinder fetuses' development, contributing to long-term health and cognitive effects.

Scientists and public health experts are working to identify the thresholds between safe and dangerous heat for maternal and prenatal health. As the planet warms, how will that affect the risks associated with pregnancy? And how can those risks be countered?

When the body tries to cool down, blood moves to the extremities to dissipate heat through the skin's surface. Some studies suggest that, in a pregnant person, this may reduce blood flow to the placenta, which in turn reduces the organ's supply of oxygen and nutrients. This is thought to contribute to low birth weight, which is associated with a heightened risk of illness and death shortly after birth, as well as cognitive and mental-health challenges later in life.

Heat stress may also spike the body's levels of cortisol beyond what they should be in a healthy pregnancy, studies in lab animals suggest. This may expose developing fetuses to more cortisol than usual, affecting some aspects of development and functions of the placenta. This mechanism is not fully understood in humans yet, though.

That said, several studies in humans support the notion of a link between heat exposure and low birth weight. Mothers who were pregnant during a 2003 heat wave in France were more than twice as likely to have a baby of low birth weight than mothers whose pregnancies did not coincide with the extreme heat. The trend was especially strong when the heat wave hit during a person's first trimester.

In a study conducted in Arizona, hotter temperatures during the third trimester were also associated with lower birth weight, said study co-author Kristin Mickelson, a social-health psychologist who studies public health at the Arizona State University.

Exposure to extreme heat has been linked to a higher chance of babies being born at a low weight. The reasons why are still being studied, however.

Preterm delivery — before 37 weeks of gestation — is one of the most concerning health outcomes that's been linked to heat, Esposito told Live Science. The risk of neurological and developmental disabilities is higher in preterm babies than in full-term babies.

"If you're born preterm, you have a heightened risk of all sorts of adversities throughout infancy, but then also extending into childhood and even longer term," said Clara Heil, health researcher at Lund University in Sweden.

Severe heat may make preterm birth more likely. A review of 70 studies suggests that the chance of preterm birth is 16% higher when a multiday heat wave hits during the final weeks of pregnancy. Regarding average temperatures across an entire pregnancy, each additional 1.8 degrees Fahrenheit (1 degree Celsius) boosts the risk of preterm birth by 5%, the same review found.

High temperatures have also been tied to early births, which occur after 37 weeks of pregnancy but before 39 completed weeks. Early birth is associated with an increased risk of hospitalizations through childhood and slower neurologic development.

A study analyzing 56 million U.S. births across two decades suggested there was about 1 more early birth per 100,000 pregnancies on days with a maximum temperature above 90 F (32.2 C), compared to 60 to 70 F (15 to 21 C). Deliveries on hot days were typically about six days earlier than anticipated, but some were up to two weeks early.

With average temperatures continuing to rise, early and premature births could become more common. The United Nations predicts that the world is set to cross the 1.5 C (2.7 F) threshold of warming compared with pre-industrial levels, likely within the next five years, and we may even surpass the 2 C (3.6 F) threshold in that timeframe.

"We can probably expect to see these temperatures, or worse, in the upcoming years," Lizbeth Burgos Ochoa, statistician at Tilburg University in the Netherlands, told Live Science.

Maternal heat exposure has also been associated with an increased risk of preeclampsia, a pregnancy complication related to blood pressure that can be fatal. In a 2011 study conducted in France, hotter temperatures during the first trimester were associated with a slightly increased risk of severe preeclampsia, although more research is needed to understand why.

Heat exposure has also been linked to other conditions of pregnancy, including gestational diabetes and hypertension, as well as increased emergency hospital admissions during pregnancy, overall.

Additional studies have highlighted potential risks for developing fetuses and newborns. For example, following a heat wave in Canada, the risk of a baby being born with critical heart defects was 6% higher, one study found. Babies with the highest risk had been exposed to temperatures of 86 F (30 C) or higher for at least 15 days during the first trimester of pregnancy.

Extreme heat waves may also raise the chance of stillbirth, according to a study conducted across six U.S. states. Four consecutive unusually hot days — exceeding the 97.5th percentile of temperature for a given county — in the last week of pregnancy was linked to a 3% increase in the risk of stillbirth.

These studies are observational, so they show links between heat and pregnancy outcomes but cannot directly show that the heat spurred the increased risks. They also don't take into account study participants' home environments or strategies for cooling off, so they don't provide a full picture of how much heat they were exposed to.

Studies suggest that both maternal, prenatal and newborn health can be negatively impacted by exposure to extreme heat during pregnancy.

Researchers are still working to understand what temperatures pregnant people should be most concerned about.

"It would be [helpful] to understand what level of heat becomes dangerous to women," Heil said. "Is there a threshold where it becomes difficult for the body to dissipate heat? And especially for the pregnant body, which is different from the non-pregnant body, to cope with that heat?"

Heil and her research team synthesized over 100 studies on heat and poor birth outcomes, such as stillbirth, preterm birth, placental abruption and preeclampsia. The studies varied considerably in that they covered different timeframes, focusing on just one trimester or all three, for instance. But in general, their results converged on a pattern: The risk of poor outcomes rose as temperatures hit about 87 F (30.5 C). At 102 F (39 C), the combined risk of poor outcomes rose by 20%.

This summer, daytime maximum temperatures in the continental U.S. averaged a record-breaking 88.6 F (31.4 C). The number of days per year over 105 F (35 C) is expected to triple in the U.S. by midcentury.

Heat's detrimental effects on birth outcomes are even more extreme for those with disadvantaged socioeconomic conditions, Burgos Ochoa noted. People with lower incomes are less likely to have air conditioning, and neighborhoods with a lower median income tend to have less green space, which contributes to trapped heat in their immediate surroundings. Expanding green spaces and adding bodies of water can help cool down neighborhoods and mitigate heat impacts on health, she said.

Heil's research team has developed a phone app for pregnant people and those who have recently given birth that forecasts the risk of heat stress and suggests actions to take to mitigate those risks, such as hydrating or changing clothing. Heil is hopeful that the app and similar early-warning systems can make a difference.

"Maternal health should be at the top [of our concerns], because it is about the next generation," Mickelson said. "If we don't take care of the mothers that are giving birth to the next generation, that's going to have long-term consequences down the road."

This article is for informational purposes only and is not meant to offer medical advice.

#5785. Spaghetti Meat Loaf

Recipe by Tyler Smith, Virginia Beach, Virginia

Spaghetti meat loaf is a mashup of family favorites, with Italian aromatics, pasta sauce and plenty of bubbly cheese.

Meat loaf has become a staple on my dinner menu for the usual reasons, mainly that it’s quick, easy and the kids will actually eat it. And while standard meat loaf is fine, it’s not super exciting—so I was happy to change things up with this spaghetti meat loaf. Recipe maverick Tyler Smith (also a parent) came up with the clever idea of mashing up two kid-friendly dinners into one. This dish has all the Italian-style flavor of spaghetti delivered in the simple, sliceable form of meat loaf.

Here’s the brilliant part: After the meat loaf is baked and sliced, it’s slathered in marinara sauce and cheese, then broiled until molten, bubbling and pizza-like. When served over a bed of spaghetti noodles, it’s got that extra panache that turns a ho-hum routine into a surprising dinner.

Preheat the oven to 375°. In a large bowl, combine the bread crumbs, milk, onion, egg, garlic, basil and salt with 1/2 cup of the Parmesan cheese. Add the ground beef. With a spatula or your hands, mix lightly but thoroughly.

Editor’s Tip: Overworking the beef can actually make for tough meat loaf, so take care to use a light touch.

Line a 9×5-inch loaf pan with parchment paper. Transfer half the meat mixture to the pan. Cut 3 mozzarella slices in half. Stack them lengthwise in the center of the meat loaf. Cover them with the remaining beef mixture. Smooth the top. Bake the meatloaf for 45 minutes

Remove the pan from the oven. Top the loaf with 1 cup of marinara sauce and the remaining 1/4 cup of Parmesan cheese.

Bake until a thermometer in the center of the loaf reads 160°, about 10 to 15 minutes longer. Let the meat loaf stand for 10 minutes before cutting it into six slices.

Editor’s Tip: Don’t be tempted to rush. Allowing the meat loaf to sit gives it th more stability and ensures that the juices properly absorb, rather than spilling out when you slice.

Preheat the broiler to high. Place the slices onto a parchment-lined baking sheet. Divide the remaining 1 cup marinara among the slices, spreading with a spoon or spatula. Add a slice of mozzarella to each piece of meat loaf. Broil them for one to two minutes, or until the cheese is melted and bubbly.

Meanwhile, cook the spaghetti according to package directions. Drain the water and toss the pasta with the remaining 1 cup of marinara.

Editor’s Tip: You can also top the noodles with sauce in individual bowls, if you prefer.

Serve the meat loaf slices over the spaghetti. Garnish plates with additional basil and Parmesan, as desired.

Store spaghetti meat loaf leftovers in the refrigerator. This meal will keep best if the meat loaf, noodles and sauce are all stored separately. The meat loaf and noodles should be used within four days; the sauce will keep for a week.

We like to reheat single portions of the meat loaf in the microwave. First, place the noodles in a bowl, sprinkle them with a teaspoon of water and heat them in 30-second blasts until they’re fairly hot. Arrange the meat loaf on a plate and sprinkle it with a bit of water. Cover it with a damp paper towel so the sauce doesn’t splatter. Heat it in 30-second increments until it’s steaming and bubbly. Top the noodles with the loaf and serve them.

If your meat loaf is crumbly or falls apart entirely when slicing, it usually means that you sliced too soon after baking. When hot from the oven, the loaf will be fragile. As it sits, the proteins will settle and the loaf will become more stable.

If you did let the loaf sit but it’s still delicate, it may mean that you didn’t mix the meat loaf quite thoroughly enough. Make a note for next time to be more thorough.

In either case, carefully spoon pieces of the loaf the best you can and top them with sauce and cheese to broil. You could also skip the broiling and simply serve the crumbly loaf like meat sauce.

This meat loaf pasta can get as fancy or simple as you like! For a weeknight dinner, pair it with crusty bread or rolls, plus an easy vegetable: Think lettuce tossed with vinaigrette or steamed broccoli. For a more elaborate meal, serve it with your favorite Italian-style side dishes like garlic bread, tomato Caprese salad or slow-cooked mushrooms.

#5783. New Balance’s Latest 990 Is Cooler Than Ever

The New Balance 990 isn’t like the other shoes. The silhouette is the Boston brand’s franchise player, the leadoff hitter, the North Star for everything New Balance does. While other sneaker brands routinely pump out new iterations of their flagship products (inevitably dulling their allure in the process), New Balance keeps the 990 sacred. New versions of the silhouette—prior to this week, there had only been six—only come around every so often. The V2 was released 16 years after the original dropped in 1982, while the V4 and V5 arrived merely three years apart. This week, for the first time since 2022’s V6, the brand is debuting the latest iteration of the sneaker that started it all. And this time, it truly might be better than ever.

“There’s a real responsibility that comes with redesigning it,” says Brian Lynn, New Balance’s global VP of lifestyle product. “The challenge is in finding the balance between preservation and evolution.” He notes that there are people for whom the 990, in whatever version is on shelves at the moment, has been their daily sneaker for decades. At the same time, each version is also always someone’s first; each has to offer something that can draw that new fan in. “People still appreciate heritage, but they’re increasingly looking for something new,” says Lynn.

For the shoe’s previous revamp, the 990v6, the brand overdelivered on the “something new.” The chunky rocker sole didn’t feel too far removed from those commonly seen on Hokas these days, and the mesh and suede paneling had a distinctly modern feel. Of all the 990s it’s the one that felt the most of its moment—which can be a good thing but, a few years down the line, risks that moment passing.

The 990v7 feels like a re-embracing of tradition, with the design improving on what the V6 set out to accomplish. It doesn’t shrink the monster sole, but it does reshape it into something a bit steadier and more old-school than the rocker build of its predecessor. It’s kitted out with a softer version of the brand’s FuelCell tech, plus a combination of ENCAP segment with a modified TPU midfoot shank, which is a very fancy way of saying it’s comfier and more orthopedically sturdy than it’s ever been. They’ve also leaned into the “embrace tradition” of it all by decking the debut colorway’s sole out in a vintage-y cream tone; the usual rich grey suede and mesh paneling we’ve come to expect from the line is there too, on a pleasantly weird and wavy new upper. The pebbled leather tongue and heel tab feel a bit luxe. It’s a real contender for the best a 990 has looked right out the gate.

According to Lin, the 990 is, as always, a labor of love for the brand. “I want people to love wearing it, to appreciate the comfort and the materials and the care that went into making it,” he says. You can see the results of New Balance’s latest edition of their flagship silhouette on November 6, when the shoe goes on sale for $200 from New Balance and select retailers.
