#5782. Global emissions slow — but world hurtles towards 2 °C of warming

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Global greenhouse-gas emissions were roughly flat from 2024 to 2025.Credit: Costfoto/NurPhoto via Getty

Despite a possible plateau in global greenhouse-gas emissions last year, average temperatures are set to hurtle past 2 °C of warming around mid-century barring a major acceleration in emissions cuts, finds the United Nations’ latest report on climate change.

The super El Niño is already wreaking havoc: here’s what’s in store

The super El Niño is already wreaking havoc: here’s what’s in store

“The world can no longer avoid overshooting the 1.5 °C limit, and the consequences are already devastating,” said outgoing UN secretary-general António Guterres in a press briefing announcing the report — an annual accounting of progress towards tackling climate change published by the UN Environment Programme (UNEP) on 21 October. Guterres pointed to extended heat waves, massive wildfires and deadly floods that have disrupted much of the world in 2026. He called for faster action from governments to cut emissions by transitioning away from fossil fuels, expanding clean electricity and reversing deforestation.

Under current policies, scientists estimate warming of 2.6 °C to 2.8 °C above pre-industrial levels this century — substantially exceeding the 2 °C upper limit of the 2015 Paris Agreement targets to avoid the most dangerous consequences of climate change. There is also a smaller, 10% chance of warming exceeding 3.4 °C on the current trajectory, which researchers say would be catastrophic. Even under a scenario where countries achieve all existing emissions pledges, the report projects 2.1 °C of warming.

Although the 1.5 °C goal is out of reach — 2026 is projected to be the second year in which temperatures have surpassed that level of warming — the report maintains it might be possible to “overshoot” that target, then bring temperatures back down through rapid decarbonization combined with a massive expansion of carbon dioxide removal.

UNEP’s assessment comes ahead of November’s COP31 climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, where governments will take stock of efforts to mitigate and adapt to climate change. “The emissions gap is now a leadership gap. And the world is running out of time and excuses to close it,” Guterres said.

There is some good news in this year’s report. Initial estimates show that global greenhouse-gas emissions in 2025 were about the same or slightly lower than in 2024. That was mainly due to a decrease in emissions from land use, particularly those from wildfires after unprecedented blazes in 2024.

That would be the first decline seen since the COVID-19 pandemic, and it could represent the start of a plateau or even a peak in emissions (see ‘Have emissions peaked?’). But researchers caution that a single year is not enough to determine if this is a lasting shift.

Source: United Nations Environment Programme

“Global emissions will have to fall several per cent before there can be any certainty that they have peaked,” says Lauri Myllyvirta, a researcher at the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air, a research organization in Helsinki. “Until then, one particularly hot summer or other cyclical development can cause a jump above the preceding peak, even if the structural trend is downward.”

Another encouraging trend is the rapid expansion of renewables. Clean-energy capacity has increased around three times faster than anticipated since 2015, and solar energy has expanded seven times faster than expected as costs have fallen.

Myllyvirta points out that even if this year doesn’t represent an emissions peak, the report projects a 5–8% drop by 2030 relative to 2025 levels. “The door to a much larger reduction than this over the next five years is open,” he says, as clean energy accelerates, fossil fuels become costlier, and because of the potential for China and India to overachieve their promised emissions cuts.

#5781. From Jeon Do-yeon to Tao Okamoto, Four Asian Actresses Giving Must-See Performances This Fall

A grieving widow rediscovers her appetite for life on a Tokyo dance floor. A working-class wife becomes the subject of a documentary by a wealthy filmmaker. A terminally ill theater director strikes up an unexpected friendship in France. And a rookie cop confronts an otherworldly threat in a remote Korean village.

The four films—Ha-chan, Shake Your Booty!, Possible Love, All of a Sudden, and Hope—could hardly be more different, but all have attracted attention on this year’s festival circuit, with major prizes at Cannes, Venice, and Sundance among their accolades. Just as notable are the standout performances by four Asian actresses at different stages of their careers: established stars Rinko Kikuchi and Jeon Do-yeon, model turned actress Tao Okamoto, and relative newcomer Hoyeon. Working in genres ranging from intimate character studies to mega spectacles, they bring richly drawn, unpredictable characters to life, making this a banner year that highlights the extraordinary versatility and vibrancy of female Asian performers working in film today.

Two decades after her Oscar-nominated Hollywood breakthrough in Alejandro González Iñárritu’s Babel, Rinko Kikuchi gets to showcase her considerable comic gifts in Ha-chan, Shake Your Booty!, released in the US last month. She plays Haru, a widow who falls for her handsome ballroom-dance instructor even as she remains haunted by her late husband (whose lingering presence takes the surreal form of a giant crow).

What follows is a heady mixture of grief, desire, fantasy, and dance, complete with elaborate musical interludes and a delirious homage to Dirty Dancing. Kikuchi makes Haru’s occasionally mortifying romantic pursuit both funny and affecting, embracing the character’s impulsive, sometimes ridiculous behavior without diminishing the depth of her loss. Inspired by director Josef Kubota Wladyka’s own mother, the film offers a refreshingly frank portrait of middle-aged female desire. And for an actress whose adventurous filmography spans the Hollywood blockbuster Pacific Rim and the offbeat indie Kumiko, the Treasure Hunter, the role draws on more of her range than ever. She shimmies, fantasizes, embarrasses herself, and ultimately makes a convincing case for the possibility of joy after heartbreak.

Jeon Do-yeon, meanwhile, brings to Possible Love the extraordinary emotional acuity that has made her one of Korean cinema’s most compelling performers. Nearly 20 years after winning best actress at Cannes for Lee Chang-dong’s Secret Sunshine, she reunites with the director for his first feature since 2018’s Burning. Opening in US theaters this Friday before arriving on Netflix on November 6, the densely layered drama explores class, marriage, and the uneasy relationship between artistic ambition and exploitation.

Jeon plays Mi-ok, whose husband hasn’t found work since losing his factory job. When a wealthy documentary filmmaker approaches the couple about chronicling the plight of laid-off workers, the two women develop an unlikely friendship, drawing their husbands into an increasingly fraught relationship that exposes the economic gulf between the couples.

Jeon is marvelous in the role, allowing flashes of envy, resentment, and longing to surface beneath Mi-ok’s cheerful, accommodating manner without diminishing her warmth or loyalty. Through subtle shifts in expression, she reveals a woman far more complex than the long-suffering wife she might initially appear to be. It’s a talent she has demonstrated throughout her career, including as a domestic worker drawn into a destructive affair in 2010’s The Housemaid.

Tao Okamoto, meanwhile, lends a gentle intensity to Ryusuke Hamaguchi’s All of a Sudden, which opens in theaters on November 25. She plays Mari, an avant-garde theater director diagnosed with terminal cancer who develops a close bond with Marie-Lou (Virginie Efira), the head of an eldercare facility outside Paris.

The three-plus-hour film unfolds largely through conversations between the two women, who move between Japanese and French as they explore mortality, caregiving, and human connection. For Okamoto, whose previous international screen credits include The Wolverine and Batman v Superman: Dawn of Justice, the role represents a significant departure. Her Mari is intellectually formidable, independent-minded, and quietly funny, refusing to let her illness define her even as she confronts the realities of dying. In one memorable scene, Mari takes to a whiteboard to explain the destructive workings of capitalism, a lengthy, potentially didactic exercise that Okamoto transforms into an unexpectedly intimate exchange.

Her joint best-actress win with Efira at Cannes this year marked a career milestone, but equally striking is the opportunity to portray a relationship between two women rooted not in rivalry or romance but in intellectual curiosity, mutual respect, and deepening affection.

For Hoyeon, Hope presented a more physically demanding challenge. The South Korean actress and former model, who shot to international fame in Squid Game, makes her feature-film debut in Na Hong-jin’s visceral science-fiction epic, which arrived in US theaters in September and was South Korea’s most expensive film ever produced. She plays Sung-ae, a rookie police officer confronting a mysterious creature terrorizing her rural village. The film is a wild, frequently grotesque spectacle that careens between horror, action, slapstick, and black comedy. Amid the mayhem, Hoyeon brings a defiant energy to a character forced to find her footing in circumstances no amount of police training could prepare her for.

In an interview with Vogue at Cannes, Hoyeon described spending six months preparing for the action sequences, including firearms training, obtaining a stick-shift driving license, and learning stunt-driving techniques. She performed much of the action herself, emerging as one of the film’s most magnetic presences.

What makes these performances exciting isn’t simply the recognition they have received, or may yet receive, during awards season. It’s their remarkable variety: four women navigating radically different worlds, each given room to be complicated, contradictory, and fully, messily human. Seeing Asian actresses offered such substantial, demanding roles is heartening; watching them respond with performances of equal depth and ambition is thrilling. The talent has never been in question; what’s needed are more roles and films worthy of it.

#5780. The Best Santoku Knife for Agile Slicing and Dicing (2025)

Most professional kitchens are dominated by chefs’ knives—long, sharp, and versatile. A good chef’s knife transforms raw ingredients through perfect cuts and detail-oriented knife work. Most home cooks, however, don’t require absolute precision for every cutting task. And when it comes to daily use utility knives, the santoku reigns supreme.

Originating in Japan around the 1940s or 1950s, santoku translates to “three uses” or “three virtues,” which might refer to meat, fish, and vegetables or to chopping, slicing, and dicing, depending on who you talk to. Santoku knives are often shorter than a chef’s knife with a relatively flat edge and a pronounced sheep’s foot point. Their overall shape lands somewhere between the nakiri, gyuto, and the deba knife—a sturdy, single-edge fish cleaver. In a modern kitchen, however, the size and shape of a santoku knife makes them easy to grab for whatever task is at hand.

While there was a bit of a santoku craze in the early 2000s when the knife shape first took off in the U.S., these days, a good santoku knife is a great utility addition to your kitchen. The best santoku knives allow you to dice vegetables, break down chickens, mince herbs, and slice onions with ease—and might just replace your current chef’s knife as your go-to blade. To help you find the best santoku for your kitchen, we tested over 30 santoku knives to find the best options at a variety of price points.

• Best santoku knife: Kazan Ginsan Nashiji Santoku• Best Western-style handle santoku knife: MAC Professional Series Santoku• A santoku knife upgrade: Misono UX10 Santoku• A worthy santoku splurge: Kazan Arare by Shibata Santoku• The best budget santoku knife: Mac Knife Superior Santoku Knife• Another affordable santoku knife: Misono Molybdenum Stainless Steel Santoku

• More on our top picks• How we chose the best santoku knives• Other santokus we liked• Santoku knives we don't recommend• FAQ

After testing a wide swath of santoku knives, we fell in love with models featuring the traditional Japanese wa-style handle for its versatility and light weight. This santoku from Kazan is handmade in Japan and features beautiful details worthy of a knife twice the price.

What we love: A great santoku knife should feel sturdy, yet nimble, and Kazan’s Ginsan Nashiji Santoku was everything we wanted in a santoku knife. It was razor-sharp out of the box and lightweight enough to slice through paper effortlessly while still retaining the cleaver-style qualities that let it power through fibrous sweet potatoes with ease. We also loved the gorgeous pear-skin style hammer marks on the spine that help with food release, along with the wavy patterns that show how the steel was tempered. It’s quickly become a go-to for any everyday task—slicing cheese, cubing potatoes, cutting a sandwich, etc. The wa-style handle shifts the weight of the knife slightly toward the blade, but also opens up a lot of grip options: it was easy to choke up for more leverage when breaking down sweet potatoes or shift to a more delicate pinch when dicing an onion. It was also the lightest knife we tested by far, adding to its numble versatility while still being supported by a sturdy spine. All in all, we loved that this knife tackled everything we threw at it while looking absolutely gorgeous.

What we'd leave: The biggest issue? Price. It’s a gorgeous handmade knife, and it is expensive. At the same time, we think this knife is absolutely worth it, and performs like a knife twice its price. The other thing to consider is that it’s made from ginsan stainless steel—a high-carbon stainless steel that’s great for edge retention but can develop discoloration from water spots if it isn’t dried completely after use. If you’re the type of person who likes to chop and go, you may want to consider our alternative picks.

Weight: 4.5 oz.Blade length: 6.7"Handle length: 5"

A true workhorse and daily driver, this santoku knife will keep chopping in your kitchen for years to come.

What we love: When it comes to performance and value, MAC’s knives are hard to beat. Our previous top pick for santoku knives was MAC’s Superior Series; on retesting, we think it’s probably worth spending a bit more for their Professional Series. The upgrade gives you a better fit and finish, along with an integrated bolster for better weight balance and hollow ground divots to help prevent food from sticking. It’s extremely sharp out of the box and easily slices through tough sweet potatoes and onions. It was comfortable to hold, and the “sub-zero tempered steel” did feel like it held its edge better than many of the knives we tested. For most people, a santoku knife is part of their daily cooking routine—if that’s the case, and a good knife can last you ten years, we feel like the upgrade to the Professional Series MAC santoku knife is absolutely worth it.

What we'd leave: While we liked the handle, we found that, compared to Japanese wa-style handles, it felt a little clunky. Because the blade goes directly into the bolster, there’s less freedom to adjust to a variety of different grip styles. That said, once we settled into the right grip for this knife, it felt like an easy extension of our hands.

Weight: 6.1 oz.Blade length: 6.5"Handle length: 5"

If you’re serious about santokus and looking for a high-quality upgrade pick, the Misono UX10 is well-loved for its 70/30 bevel and incredible slicing ability.

What we love: We’re big fans of this family-run Japanese brand’s knives across the board for their superior agility, precision, and edge retention (their chef’s knife is also one of our top picks). The UX10 santoku has an ultra-thin blade that not only offers an insanely sharp edge out of the box, but also retains that sharpness even after prolonged use. And because it’s made of stain-resistant Swedish steel, it’s not as prone to discoloration as the high-carbon steel blade of the Mac. We also love that its handle tapers as it gets towards the blade, which makes it very comfortable to hold—no hand cramps here.

What we'd leave: Besides the price (around $220 at the time of writing), the one major thing to note about the Misono is that its 70/30 asymmetrical bevel. That doesn’t make it a lesser knife, but it does make it more difficult to sharpen at home. It also means you’ll need to change the orientation of the bevels after purchasing it if you’re left-handed. That’s because the two sides of the blade are sharpened to different angles, one shallower and one more acute.

Weight: 5.6 oz.Blade length: 7”Blade height: 5”

If you want an unbelievably sharp, nimble, and beautiful santoku knife, this santoku checks every box we could possibly imagine.

What we love: When we tested Japanese knives, there was a clear winner we fell in love with. The Kazan Arare by Shibata line also offers a santoku knife, and we were just as in love with it as its gyuto sibling. To start, it features a hand-hammered spine that removes weight and keeps food from sticking while looking incredible. It also has a near-perfect santoku shape: a mostly straight edge with a tall heel and a severe sheep’s foot tip for precision cuts. It rivaled the Misono UX10 for sharpness by gliding effortlessly through paper and slicing through onion layers with zero resistance, and it’s edge geometry is so thin that even tough sweet potatoes were easy to slice without much effort. We love that it has an easy-to-maintain 50/50 bevel on its edge, and the ebony handle with a decorative ring felt exquisite in our hand.

What we'd leave: The biggest knock against this knife is the price—it’s our most expensive top pick, though we feel like a knife of this quality would usually be priced in the $300-$400 range. The other thing to take note of is that it’s made from SG2 steel, which is great for edge retention but can show water spots more than other high-carbon stainless steels.

Weight: 5.5 oz.Blade length: 6.5"Handle length: 5.5"

A classic santoku that’s been made by MAC since the 1940s and one of the earliest examples of the knife’s design.

What we love: Like the other knives in the brand’s lineup, Mac’s santoku strikes the right balance between performance and price. At $80 at the time of writing, it really can’t be beat. It’s razor-sharp out of the box and has a lightweight, well-balanced feel that makes it easy to maneuver.

In our testing, the high-carbon-steel blade was equally adept at slicing through hardy sweet potatoes as it was delicate herbs. In long-term testing, it’s held up as a regular go-to knife for our testers. When it does come time to sharpen, the Mac’s blade easily comes back to life after several passes on a whetstone (or through a high-quality electric knife sharpener).

What we'd leave: In our latest round of testing, the Professional Series MAC handle beat out this model for comfort and durability. But if you’re curious about picking up a santoku knife and don’t think it’s going to be a daily driver, this is a perfect option.

Weight: 5.5 oz.Blade length: 6.5"Handle length: 5"

Misono Molybdenum Stainless Steel Santoku

Misono’s Molybdenum Stainless Steel Santoku offers nearly the same sharpness as the UX10 model at a more affordable price point.

What we love: Misono’s UX10 line is legendary amongst chefs for its precision slicing—their Molybdenum line is a more affordable version that offers just as sharp an edge (though it lacks the same edge retention of the higher-end model). We loved how sharp and versatile this knife was, and at around $100 at the time of testing, it almost won out as a top pick overall for its performance to value ratio. Its tall heel gives you plenty of knuckle clearance while giving you enough steel for a variety of pinch grips, and it’s pronounced tip was perfect for precise cuts when dicing an onion.

What we'd leave: Part of this knife’s cutting ability comes from its 70/30 edge bevel, making it harder to maintain at home with practice sharpening. It’s also made from a less hard steel, meaning it won’t hold it’s edge as well as the UX10 option.

Weight: 5.2 oz.Blade length: 7"Handle length: 4.75"

We tested santoku knives similarly to how we tested chef’s knives: That is, by cutting up a lot of produce. We put them to work on sweet potatoes, raw onions, and herbs to see how they fared with both tough and delicate ingredients. We also spent time examining each knife for build quality and overall design, and took note of how each one felt to hold and use for long periods of time. We evaluated each knife according to the following criteria:

How does the knife feel to hold and use? How heavy is it, how comfortable is the handle, and how responsive is the blade?

Santokus are meant to be agile and versatile, so we were looking for a lightweight knife that was comfortable to hold, but still felt study enough to handle tougher tasks. We also paid attention to the knives’ responsiveness, which refers to how much you feel the blade react in your hand. The more responsive the knife, the more control you’ll feel over it.

We looked at how well-made each knife was, paying close attention to the finishes, the transition between the blade and handle, and any unique design features.

How sharp is the knife? How does it fare in our tests?

We looked for blades that were razor sharp out of the box, able to cut through anything we threw at them with ease and precision. Given that santokus are designed for versatility, we wanted a knife that could cleanly cube sweet potatoes, cut onions into even, thin slices, and produce a pile of finely chopped (not crushed) herbs.

This was a truly beautiful santoku with a comfortable handle, gorgeous hand-hammered details, and a beautiful Damascus pattern. We just preferred the general blade shape and height of the other knives we tested. A perfectly great example of a santoku, however, and we think most people would be happy with its performance and style at this price point.

The Shun knife is another solid option for those drawn to the look of Damascus steel blades. Like most Classic Shun knives we’ve tested, it had an incredible edge out of the box. Our big issue was with the overall size of the knife and its handle—both are a bit large for a daily-use utility knife.

Almost identical to the knife listed above, this version has channels cut into the blade to prevent food from sticking and reduce weight. Our evaluation remains the same, however—sharp, beautiful, but a little unwieldy compared to our top picks.

Wüsthof Classic 7-Inch Hollow Edge Santoku

We liked this well-made knife from the German brand Wüsthof for its super-sharp, high-carbon-steel blade, full-tang construction, triple-riveted handle, and excellent performance across all our tests. It’s a solid choice if you want a santoku knife with better stain resistance and slightly softer steel that won’t chip as easily.

Lots of people love Global knives for their middle-of-the-road price and balanced, lightweight feel. They’ve historically fared well in our tests, and the 7" santoku was no exception. Our testers found the handle’s slim, ergonomic shape and seamless transition into the blade comfortable to grip, but users with larger hands may prefer a handle with a bit more bulk. If you’re a Global stan, we’re confident you’ll love this knife, but at nearly $150 at the time of writing, we feel like it’s just a little pricey compared to our other top picks.

Miyabi makes truly beautiful Damascus-patterned blades with elaborate handles, but we find their price tag is just a big higher than we’d like on average. This Birchwood SG2 santoku was light, nimble, sharp, and truly beautiful to behold. The only issue? We didn’t feel like it was as comfortable as our splurge picks, and it costs over $100 more than our most expensive pick. Most people will love using this knife, but its value is more tied to appearance and materials than cutting ability.

The Miyabi Artisan is a beautiful knife, with a rosewood pakkawood handle and shiny hammered steel blade. It was very sharp and comfortable to hold, but we didn’t feel it was worth the $225 price tag, considering it performed similarly to the Mac. That said, if you’re drawn to it for aesthetic reasons and willing to splurge, you can trust it will serve you well.

This is another stunning Miyabi knife that features a Damascus steel blade and a black pakkawood handle. It performed well in testing and is reasonably priced for its Damascus pattern. We just preferred other knives for performance and handle comfort at this price point.

The Victorinox Swiss Classic was our previous budget pick. It’s incredibly lightweight at just 4.6 ounces, very sharp out of the box, has a granton edge (those are the divots you see on lots of santokus) meant to discourage food from sticking to the blade, and impressed us throughout all of our testing. Its plastic handle makes it feel quite cheap, which we didn’t mind when it was under $40. Still, it is the cheapest knife we recommend, so if you’re on a tight budget this is a decent option.

Tojiro’s knives are sharp and well-made, but often lack the polished details of competitors like MAC and Misono. Now that Tojiro’s prices have gone up, we’re recommending them less and less. It’s still a sharp and versatile santoku knife, we just think you’re better off spending just a bit more for a higher level of fit and finish.

This budget-friendly knife will get the job done if you’re in the market for a knife around $40, but it was too heavy and uncomfortable to grip for us to recommend it.

This knife from Misen was far too heavy to grant us the agility and precision we were looking for in a santoku.

Henckels Classic Precision Hollow Edge Rocking Santoku Knife

This knife has an odd shape for a santoku, mostly because it’s a kiritsuke knife—not a santoku. The curved edge was very effective for chopping herbs, but we preferred a flatter edge for other tasks like slicing through sweet potatoes and onions.

Zwilling Twin Signature Hollow Edge Santoku Knife

We thought the balance between the handle and the blade felt off, and that the handle was too bulky. We also wish it were sharper.

Our initial paper-slicing test had us impressed with the sharpness of Miyabi’s Hisho santoku, but it met too much resistance when cutting sweet potatoes for this price point.

This knife was too small to be truly useful in the kitchen. For a knife this size, we recommend looking at a petty knife instead.

While beautiful, the extra knife material needed to achieve its Damascus pattern made it heavy and unwieldy. We also didn’t like the handle shape, and it was expensive.

We were surprised at how dull this knife felt out of the box, given how impressed we were with Shun’s standard Classic line. Again, this knife’s dual core added weight and bulk to a knife shape that should be more agile, and it’s priced too high.

We loved the look and handle feel of Shun’s Premier Santoku, but it lacked sharpness. For this price, you can get a sharper knife made by hand in a small studio.

Too large, bulky, and heavy for a santoku knife—it also struggled to cut as cleanly as we’d like, and is one of the most expensive knives we tested.

We’re a little torn on this one—we like the shape and the gorgeous Damascus-patterned steel, and it cut fairly well in our tests. Overall, we just think the price is a little high compared to our top picks and wouldn’t recommend this knife over any of them.

What’s the difference between a santoku knife and a chef’s knife?

Santoku knives are designed to be all-around utility knives—they often have the same height as a chef’s knife, but a shorter, straighter edge and a very pronounced sheep’s foot tip. While chefs’ knives are designed for pull-through slicing and rock chopping on a cutting board, santoku knives are mostly designed for push cuts and fine point work.

Santoku knives combine the best aspects of nakiris, gyutos, and petty knives—they’re designed to be a middle-of-the-road utility knife that can chop and cut while being nimble enough for fine tasks. A great santoku knife is lightweight, but has a big enough blade to tackle everyday cutting tasks.

Santoku knives are great all-around utility knives for any home cook at any skill level. Some economical kitchens can even make do with just a santoku knife. However, if you already have a chef’s knife, paring knife, utility knife, etc., adding a santoku knife might feel a bit like overkill.

#5779. The 40 Best Christmas Gifts for Men Who Never Shop for Themselves

Your go-to list for the guy who has—or says he has—everything.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Guys can be hard to shop for, whether they’re rugged, flannel-wearing ones who spend time tinkering with things in the garage or inquisitive film buffs who’d rather spend money on movie tickets than new clothes. No matter which profile he fits, his closet is likely long overdue for an overhaul.

Maybe he refuses to buy himself a new pair of hiking boots, even though the ones he has are held together with duct tape. Or maybe he’s still drinking from his favorite whiskey glass, even though it’s chipped and on the verge of breaking in half. #5778. 27 People Are Sick & 5 Hospitalized In A Multistate Raw Oyster Recall

The affected oysters were shipped to restaurants and retailers across the country.

After 27 documented cases and five hospitalizations, the CDC, FDA, and public health officials are investigating a multistate Salmonella outbreak. Thus far, officials have linked the outbreak to raw oysters, which are now being recalled.

Little Wicomico Oyster Company has voluntarily recalled oysters harvested from the Little Wicomico River in Virginia and contacted customers who purchased its products. The affected whole (shellstock) oysters were harvested between September 18, 2026, and October 8, 2026.

The recalled oysters were distributed to restaurants and retailers in 15 states and Washington, D.C., including New York, New Jersey, Pennsylvania, Florida, Texas, and Virginia, and may also have been distributed elsewhere.

Because of the outbreak, the Virginia Department of Health has temporarily shut down the Little Wicomico River harvest area. Oysters from other Virginia growing areas are not part of this recall.

The CDC issued a Food Safety Alert after an investigation found that 27 people across 12 states developed Salmonella infections linked to raw oysters. Five people have been hospitalized, and no deaths have been reported.

Salmonella infections can cause symptoms including diarrhea, stomach cramps, and fever. Symptoms typically begin between six hours and six days after infection, and most people recover within four to seven days. Children under five, adults 65 and older, and people with weakened immune systems are at greater risk of severe illness.

The Virginia Department of Health also notes that eating raw or undercooked oysters carries an inherent risk of foodborne illness.

Consumers should not eat the recalled oysters, and businesses should not sell or serve them. Instead, the oysters should be thrown away or returned.

It’s also important to thoroughly wash and sanitize any surfaces or containers that may have come into contact with the recalled oysters. Anyone who may have eaten them and is experiencing symptoms should contact a healthcare provider.

#5777. Baltimore Yat Gaw Mein

Yat gaw mein, also known as Baltimore yak or dirty yak, is Baltimore carryout comfort food at its finest. A Chinese American remix of Beijing’s zha jiang mian, the dish dates back to the late 19th century, when Chinese immigrants started settling in the city. Setting up shop in traditionally Black and working-class neighborhoods, Chinese cooks adapted their recipes to locally available ingredients, swapping fermented pastes for a simpler, soy-based gravy. Over time, something new emerged: savory wheat noodles, stir-fried with shrimp, beef, and vegetables and punctuated with a hard-boiled egg. Today you can find the beloved dish at counter spots around town like Jimmy’s Carryout, Danny’s, and Pimlico Chinese Carry Out.

In this at-home version from Steve Chu, the chef behind Baltimore’s Ekiben, smoky wok-charred noodles are tossed with umami-packed soy sauce and oyster sauce, rich fat, and sweet caramelized onions. What makes the dish “dirty” is the flavor that develops when soy sauce, sugar, and aromatics scorch against the hot metal of the wok, creating deep savory notes balanced by subtle sweetness. Be sure to keep the wok on high heat and stir frequently, since this allows the wok hei—which translates to “the breath of a wok” and refers to that elusive smokiness—to work its magic. The finishing squeeze of ketchup might sound unusual, but it delivers the acidic punch that makes the dish complete.

#5776. BYD’s electric luxury GT will get a major update for Europe in 2027

BYD used the Denza Z9 GT to showcase its latest breakthrough technology, including its 5-minute Flash Charging. Next year, the luxury GT will get another major update in Europe.

After unveiling the new Z9 GT in China earlier this year, BYD’s luxury sub-brand Denza claimed it was the “world’s longest pure electric driving range electric vehicle,” offering a CLTC range of up to 1,036 km (643 miles).

BYD launched the electric GT in Europe in July, its first vehicle in Europe to use its tri-motor e3 platform, second-generation Blade Batteries and Flash Charging Technology.

The Denza Z9 GT kicked off “Europe’s Flash Charging revolution,” the company said, enabling drivers to charge up just as quickly as filling a gas tank.

Next year, BYD announced that Denza vehicles in Europe will receive an upgrade to its “God’s Eye” intelligent driving assistance system, adding “a new level” of urban driving ability.

Starting with the Z9 GT in 2027, Denza vehicles will receive City Navigation on Driving Assistant (CNOA) through an over-the-air (OTA) software update.

While average ADAS systems can handle straight-line driving on open roads, BYD said CNOA can manage point-to-point navigation in dense city driving.

The update will use the vehicle’s existing hardware, including a LiDAR, 12 ultrasonic radar sensors, three millimeter-wave radar sensors, and 11 cameras.

Once installed, the vehicle can automatically stop at red lights, yield at roundabouts, drive at the posted speed limit, make left turns, and change lanes around double-parked vehicles. It can also detect pedestrians, cyclists, and other objects in narrow city streets.

Stella Li, BYD’s executive vice president, said that “We’re excited to fulfill our promise to bring the benefits of God’s Eye, including City Navigation on Driving Assistant, to the Z9GT.”

The hardware is already pre-installed, and the software is expected to be available in 2027 via an OTA update.

In Europe and the UK, the Denza Z9 GT is available in purely electric (EV) and plug-in hybrid (PHEV) powertrains.

The all-electric version uses a 122.49 kWh battery pack and three electric motors, delivering a combined 1,140 hp (850 kW) and 892 lb-ft (1,210 Nm) of torque. Under WLTP conditions, the Denza Z9 GT offers up to 599 km (372 miles) of range.

Yo BMW and other legacy automakers. The Denza Z9 shooting brake is a beautiful design. Could use a bit more "hood" and a 3/4 size variant, but as a plug in EREV or pure EV it shows what can be done with a little effort. Where can I place my US order?

With up to 1,500 kW of charging power, BYD’s Flash Charging stations offer what it calls “Ready in 5, Full in 9, Cold Add 3.”

In 5 minutes, the Denza Z9 GT can charge from 10% to 70%. To go from 10% to 97%, it takes just 9 minutes, and even in temperatures as low as -30°C, it takes only 3 minutes longer, replenishing the battery from 20% to 97% in 12 minutes.

The Denza GT is 5,195 mm long, 1,990 mm wide, and 1,480 mm tall, with a wheelbase of 3,125 mm, or about the size of the Porsche Panamera and Taycan.

In Europe, the Denza Z9 GT EV starts at €115,000 ($130,000), including VAT. In the UK, it’s priced from £105,000 ($139,000) for the EV variant. The plug-in hybrid (DM) starts at £95,000 ($126,000).

#5775. Gnocchi alla Sorrentina

Gnocchi alla Sorrentina is a cheesy, bubbly comfort-food dinner bake that makes ordinary weeknights feel extra special.

Some recipes earn a permanent spot in your weeknight rotation because they’re just so stinking easy, and gnocchi alla Sorrentina is one of them. It’s the kind of comfort food you’d find among Nonna’s favorite Italian casseroles, but it comes together with surprisingly little effort. Just layer tender gnocchi, tangy tomato sauce, fresh basil and plenty of melty mozzarella in a baking dish, then let the oven do the rest.

The basic recipe is easy to adapt, too. Add Italian sausage for a heartier dinner, stir sauteed veggies into the sauce or keep it simple for a meatless meal. And leftovers (if you happen to have them) reheat beautifully, so tomorrow’s lunch is already taken care of.

Preheat the oven to 350°F. Bring a large pot of salted water to a boil and cook the gnocchi according to the package directions, just until tender. Drain the water.

Meanwhile, in a large cast-iron or other heavy skillet, cook the garlic in oil over medium heat until it’s browned, about two to three minutes. Stir in the crushed tomatoes, basil and salt, and cook the sauce until it’s slightly reduced, 8 to 10 minutes.

Spread one-third of the tomato sauce in a 2-quart baking dish. Top it with half of the gnocchi.

Add half of the mozzarella cheese. Repeat with another third of the sauce, the remaining gnocchi and mozzarella.

Finish the layers with the remaining tomato sauce.

Bake the gnocchi until they’re golden brown and bubbly, about 28 to 32 minutes. Sprinkle the casserole with Parmesan cheese before serving it, if desired

Let the baked gnocchi cool slightly, then cover the dish tightly or transfer leftovers to an airtight container. Refrigerate it for up to four days. Reheat individual portions in the microwave or warm larger portions, covered, in a 350° oven until they’re heated through.

Gnocchi alla Sorrentina isn’t an ideal freezer recipe. The gnocchi can become a little gummy after being frozen and thawed, and fresh mozzarella can release moisture that makes the dish watery. For the best texture, make it fresh and refrigerate any leftovers to enjoy within a few days.

Yes! Gnocchi alla Sorrentina can be assembled up to one day ahead of time to be baked just before serving. Cook the gnocchi, make the sauce and layer everything in the baking dish, then cover and refrigerate it.

When you’re ready to bake, let the dish sit at room temperature while the oven preheats, then bake as directed. You may need to add a few extra minutes to the baking time to make sure the center is heated through.

Gnocchi alla Sorrentina translates to “gnocchi in the style of Sorrento,” which refers to a town on Italy’s Amalfi Coast. The classic dish is a combination of pillowy potato dumplings, tomato sauce, fresh basil and mozzarella, all baked together until bubbly and golden.

Yes, you can absolutely make this gnocchi alla Sorrentina recipe with frozen gnocchi. Just cook it according to the package directions, then drain it well before layering it with the sauce and cheese.

Honestly, you don’t need much alongside gnocchi alla Sorrentina; it’s a pretty satisfying meal all by itself. But if you want to round things out, serve it with a crisp Caesar salad and garlic bread for an easy Italian-inspired dinner. A simple green salad or roasted vegetable medley also pairs nicely with the cheesy, saucy gnocchi.

#5774. 'Birdman' copper plate: A 700-year-old depiction of a Native American warrior that may be a woman holding a weapon and a severed head

Discovered in an Indigenous grave in Georgia nearly 150 years ago, the "Birdman" copper plate may actually depict a woman ceremonially dressed as a warrior.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This hammered copper artifact depicts an Indigenous person wearing a bird costume and holding a severed head and a mace.

What it is: A copper plate with a hammered decoration

Where it is from: Cahokia Mounds, Illinois

This copper plate was discovered in the late 19th century in a prehistoric Native American burial at the Etowah Indian Mounds archaeological site in Georgia, but it was likely made hundreds of miles away at Cahokia Mounds in Illinois. While the decorative plate showcases long-distance trade and a shared ritual culture among early Indigenous groups in the southeastern United States, the nature of the human figure depicted on the artifact has been debated for over a century.

The "Birdman" relic is one of two hammered copper plates that Smithsonian archaeologist John P. Rogan unearthed in the 1880s while digging in a burial mound at Etowah, a centuries-old village occupied by thousands of Native Americans from 1000 to 1550. Etowah was part of the "mound-building," or Mississippian, phase of Indigenous occupation; other famous sites include Moundville in Alabama and Cahokia in Illinois. These groups had extensive trade networks and shared a common artistic and ceremonial tradition known as the Southeastern Ceremonial Complex.

This copper artifact, known as Rogan Plate 1 or the "Birdman" plate, is in the collection of the Smithsonian National Museum of Natural History in Washington, D.C. The plate is roughly 11 inches (28 centimeters) tall and had been buried with a high-status person who likely died at Etowah in the mid-14th century. But experts believe the copper plate was an heirloom made decades earlier, at Cahokia, and traded south to Etowah.

A human figure dominates the copper plate with its elaborate bird costume. The person carries a severed head by the hair in their left hand, while their right hand holds a club or mace. The figure also wears a pleated skirt or loincloth that may have been made from human scalps. This bird-human figure is commonly depicted in the Southeastern Ceremonial Complex and has been called a dancing warrior, eagle warrior, eagle dancer, hawk dancer, falcon dancer and falcon warrior. But more often, the figure is called Birdman or Morning Star, a prehistoric Mississippian hero associated with agriculture and rebirth.

Archaeologists' interpretations of the Birdman plaque from Etowah have long assumed that the represented figure is a male warrior. In a 2004 study, for example, archaeologist Adam King wrote that "this plaque would have served as an emblem of office awarded to a chieftain, and would have accompanied him as a sign of his strength among the ancestral spirits."

But the sex of the skeleton in the Etowah burial is unclear, and there were high-status graves of both men and women at Etowah, archaeologist Billie Follensbee and art historian Katie Buford wrote in a 2017 study. Although experts previously assumed that both Rogan Plate 1 and Rogan Plate 2 represented male warriors, Follensbee and Buford found that the figures have slender bodies with prominent right breasts, suggesting the "Birdman" may have actually been female.

"A female bird-human warrior could have represented a gender variant individual in the form of the well-known Native American phenomenon of the 'manly-hearted woman,' a woman who has assumed the usually male role and the garb of a combat soldier," Follensbee and Buford wrote. In addition to the historic term "manly-hearted woman," third-gender or gender-variant individuals in Indigenous North America are often referred to as "two-spirit," a broad term Indigenous people coined in 1990 to describe people who take on gender-nonconforming roles.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

The commemoration of a two-spirit individual on a copper artifact, as well as the use of the artifact as a high-status grave good, "not only demonstrates the Native American cultural acceptance of such gender variant roles but also further serves to celebrate the elite status and achievements of these 'manly-hearted women,'" Follensbee and Buford wrote.

For more stunning archaeological discoveries, check out our Astonishing Artifacts archives.

Can you put together last week's Astonishing Artifact?

Kristina Killgrove is a staff writer at Live Science with a focus on archaeology and paleoanthropology news. Her articles have also appeared in venues such as Forbes, Smithsonian, and Mental Floss. Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

#5772. 32 Serving Platters That Are As Centerpiece-Worthy As the Main Course

I’m not going to argue that the manner in which a dish is plated and served actually affects the way it tastes (though I know some will). That said, I’m of the belief that presentation does affect the experience of eating a meal. A simple roast chicken becomes that much more impressive when it’s carved neatly and arranged on a pretty scallop-edged platter, just as a plate of spaghetti pomodoro somehow becomes restaurant-worthy when the plate in question is a piece of hand-painted stoneware.

Whether you’re looking to elevate your Sunday family suppers or deck out this year's holiday tablescape with a conversation-starting piece, these platters may just convince you to ditch your basic white serveware for something with a bit more personality. Read on for a list of plates that’ll set the tone for your whole meal, no matter what kind of vibe you’re curating.

If your style leans minimalist and modern, these monochrome options will make an elegant addition to your serveware collection. Add a pop of color to the dinner table with a glossy glazed number, keep things neutral with a black marble tray, or go classic and Michelin-worthy with a Jono Pandolfi platter featuring the renowned ceramist’s signature exposed clay rim.

For something more intricate, look to a hand-painted stoneware platter. Transport your dinner guests to Italy with a piece from the iconic Ginori 1735, or opt for something more graphic like a checkered platter from Maison Flâneur.

Hand Painted Serving Platter, Tulip Crown

Helecho Hand-Painted Ceramic Serving Platters (Set of 2)

A decorative edge is an elegant way to zhuzh up an otherwise plain platter, and it has the benefit of being visible even when that platter is full of food. Pretty ruffles, bead-like dots, or orderly scallops? Take your pick.

These platters will appeal to anyone who favors asymmetry and organic forms, and they’d be equally at home as part of a coffee table grazing spread as on a formal dinner party table. Use the contours of the Wavy Tapas Platter to accommodate crackers, cheese, and clusters of olives, or go for the Nuno Splatter Stoneware Serve Platter if you want a classic shape with an expressive paint job.

Any of the platters on this list would be a welcome addition to this year's holiday table, but here are some picks that feel particularly fitting as vessels for bronzed turkeys, glossy hams, stacks of flaky biscuits, and mountains of crispy maple-glazed brussels sprouts or carrots.

Etu Home Vintage English Scalloped Bread Board

Rectangular Lead Earthenware Serving Dish

I wouldn’t recommend using most of the above for any outdoor entertaining—it’s not worth the risk of turning your gorgeous new hand-painted stoneware into a pile of shards. Instead, opt for a lightweight shatterproof material like enamelware, melamine, or wood to serve this summer’s helpings of grilled kebabs and sliced watermelon. I’m particularly fond of the glossy serveware from UK-based brand Falcon Enamelware, and I was delighted to find out that contrary to what I’d always been told about enamelware, Falcon’s products are all dishwasher-safe.

#5771. The Vogue Business Beauty Trend Tracker

The Vogue Business Beauty Tracker, produced in collaboration with Spate, tracks the top five brands, trends and ingredients that are driving the most significant positive change in year-on-year growth that have also demonstrated consistent upward growth historically. Each week, we’ll spotlight a different beauty category and the top five beauty hashtags on TikTok.

In haircare, products that combine scalp support, deep nourishment, and targeted cleansing are trending in search. Peptide hair serums cater to those seeking lightweight formulas that support fuller-looking hair, with searches associated primarily with The Ordinary’s Multi-Peptide Serum for Hair Density, while hair thinning serums points to a broader focus on density and scalpcare. Rice water hair spray highlights interest in a convenient leave-in format for an ingredient valued for adding strength and shine. Cholesterol hair treatment reflects demand for rich, deep-conditioning masks that restore moisture and softness, and chlorine removal shampoo signals demand for clarifying formulas that remove chlorine build-up post-swim.

The portability and ease of scar sticks signal demand for convenient, targeted solutions, while liquid microneedling highlights appetite for professional-level collagen stimulation and enhanced absorption without in-clinic procedures. Growing interest in milk sunscreens signals demand for high-performance UV protection that feels lightweight, skincare-forward, and multifunctional. There’s also growth in matte lip balms, with Celine launching refillable matte lip balms accompanied by a special installation at Harrods on March 2.

Trending skincare brands reflect a shift toward clinically grounded, barrier-focused skin health paired with sustainability and device-led innovation. Genabelle and Tosowoong, both K-beauty brands, exemplify this movement: Genabelle develops science-backed, microbiome and PDRN-driven formulas designed to repair the skin barrier, while Tosowoong targets brightening, anti-aging, acne, and dehydration. Across haircare, consumers are demonstrating an increasing desire for products that align with holistic wellness approaches.

Experimentation and personalisation usher in a new era of scent exploration. The year-on-year growth for discovery sets, pheromone perfumes, and oil-based and roll-on scents suggest consumers are seeking variety as well as the ability to sample before committing to a full-size purchase. Discovery sets are averaging at 1,600 monthly searches, which reflects a strong desire for value and exploration in scent choices. As sample-size scents witness positive growth, fragrance variety in the form of roll-on and oil-based products follow closely behind in a shift towards long-lasting and portable fragrance solutions that appeal to convenience and a desire for subtle scent choices. As consumers seek variety, they are more receptive to experimenting with fragrances that enhance personal allure and attraction (for themselves and others). The rise in pheromone perfumes align with a consumer desire for products that boast confidence-boosting properties. Chocolate nails reference the “chocolate glazed donut nails” trend coined by Hailey Bieber’s nail artist Zola Ganzorigt. Searches for chrome, iridescent and shimmery finishes supports modern nail enthusiasts’ desire for nails that shine.

Top makeup trends, such as goth eyebrows, passport makeup and chav makeup are highly visual looks that first gained popularity on TikTok, demonstrating the social platform’s influence in driving global aesthetics. The search for two tone lips offers an opportunity for brands to explore new lip styles, while searches for bleached eyebrows alongside skin tones and hair types reveal that consumers are looking for visual examples of this trend across different demographics.

The top search queries driving makeup indicate an interest in complexion products such as foundation and blush, reminding brands to highlight complexion products for the summer. The growth in searches for Florence by Mills and Rare Beauty reveals ongoing interest in celebrity-led lines. Kiko Milano is gaining traction within US search, demonstrating new opportunities for the Italian brand among American consumers.

The top growing makeup benefits reveal that consumers are looking for products that leave them with a bright and healthy look. Searches for water-based items also indicate that consumers are becoming savvy with product formulations, revealing a preference for different formula nuances – something for brands to consider when it comes to product positioning.

There is a growing trend among consumers to embrace more immediate solutions for skin concerns, seen in the form of nanoneedling, lasers and facials. Consumers are also showing preference for multi-balms due to their functionality and ease of use. The rising interest in snail serum, along with the snail facial, indicates that consumers are willing to overlook foreign textures and potentially uncomfortable sensations in exchange for results, such as intense hydration.

Across these top-growing skincare brands, consumers are looking for reviews. Searches for these brands are driven more by the brand name than by a specific product, highlighting the significance of branding and visibility for today's consumer.

The top growing skincare ingredients reveal that hydration and anti-aging concerns are top of mind for consumers. Many of these high-growth ingredients are being driven by branded searches, highlighting the importance of brands creating products that prominently feature key ingredients.

There is a growing trend among consumers to seek out haircuts that are considered more masculine and involve shorter lengths that are closer to the scalp. Consumers are using hair gems to express their unique style and are also using hair tools, such as volumising irons, to add flair to their hairstyles. Additionally, there is growing interest in pre-washing hair, indicating a willingness among consumers to rethink traditional routines.

Consumers are showing interest in wigs, demonstrated by the enduring popularity of premium human-hair brands such as Hairvivi and Recool Hair. Concerns related to hair loss are also prominent, particularly when searching for Hims, which offers both prescription and nonprescription hair loss treatments. There is also growing demand for products that address root cover-up and grey hair, as seen in brand searches for Arey and Color Wow.

Searches encompass a variety of forms but oils are emerging as a prominent format. Notably, there has been a growing fascination with holistic and botanical formulas and ingredients, as indicated by the increased interest in ayurveda in search trends. Many of the popular ingredients are searched for their ability to support hair growth and density.

Sustained riser: Steady growth over the past years, these trends are safe bets.

Seasonal riser: Seasonal trends that are likely to come back even stronger.

“Very low / No competition” indicates there are little to no brand searches alongside a trend.

“Low competition” indicates that there are a few brand searches alongside a trend.

“Medium competition” indicates that there are some brand searches alongside a trend.

“High competition” indicates that there are several brand searches alongside a trend.

“Very high competition” indicates there are many brand searches.

#5770. KFC Is Replacing Pepsi With Coca-Cola—Here’s When The Switch Happens

KFC is officially breaking up with Pepsi, and after nearly 40 years together, I think we can call this a pretty significant split.

The fried chicken chain announced that it’s switching from PepsiCo to Coca-Cola products at its U.S. restaurants, ending a beverage partnership that dates all the way back to 1986. Starting in January 2027, KFC will begin replacing its current drink lineup with Coca-Cola beverages, with the transition expected to be complete nationwide by spring 2027.

That means customers can expect to see familiar PepsiCo drinks like Pepsi, Mountain Dew, and Starry replaced with Coca-Cola favorites like Coke, Sprite, and Fanta. So if you’ve spent years washing down your KFC chicken with a Pepsi, you might want to enjoy a few more while you still can.

You may be surprised to learn that PepsiCo purchased KFC in 1986, making Pepsi the chain’s exclusive beverage partner. Even after PepsiCo sold off its restaurant business in 1997, eventually becoming part of Yum! Brands, the soda relationship continued.

Now, KFC is ready for something different.

“I’m incredibly excited to bring KFC and Coca-Cola together. These are two originals, with deep consumer love,” Catherine Tan-Gillespie, president of KFC U.S., said in a statement.

The change is part of KFC’s larger comeback strategy, which has included bringing back fan favorites like potato wedges and popcorn chicken, introducing new value offerings, and refreshing its menu and marketing.

There is some good news for Pepsi loyalists, though. The switch only applies to KFC’s U.S. restaurants, and PepsiCo is expected to remain a beverage partner in international markets.

Personally, I’m more interested in seeing what KFC does with its expanded drink possibilities. Fried chicken and an ice-cold Coke sound pretty good already, but if this partnership leads to some interesting new beverages, I’m listening.

#5769. Copycat Panera Three-Cheese Lasagna Soup

Recipe by Lauren Habermehl, Pewaukee, Wisconsin

Our copycat Panera three-cheese lasagna soup recipe is just as delicious as Panera's new seasonal offering, but you won't have to leave the house.

I don’t frequent fast-food or fast-casual restaurants often, but when the day calls for a quick, on-the-go meal, my family keeps Panera in our back pocket. While my kids are loyal to Panera’s mac and cheese, I always look forward to the seasonal offerings—and the new Panera three-cheese lasagna soup is my newest favorite. This copycat recipe is my attempt to recreate the hearty, creamy and satisfying soup so I can have it at home year-round.

It starts with a base of garlic, onions and ground pork, and is seasoned with a menagerie of bold Italian herbs and spices. The meat and onions are bathed in a rich tomato broth enriched with cream, ricotta cheese and plenty of Parmesan. The cozy soup is finished with al dente mafalda pasta and a sprinkle of Asiago cheese over every serving.

At home, I like to pair this copycat recipe with a fresh garden salad and a loaf of crusty Dutch oven bread for dipping and dunking. And for an extra-hearty meal, I like to serve this soup in a crusty bread bowl.

Bring a large pot of water to a boil. Cook the pasta according to its package directions. Drain the water and set the pasta aside.

Heat the butter in a Dutch oven or stock pot over medium heat. Add the onion and cook it until it’s softened, three to four minutes, stirring occasionally. Add the garlic and cook until it’s fragrant, 30 to 60 seconds.

Add the pork, Italian seasoning, fennel, salt, onion powder, garlic powder, pepper and red pepper flakes. Cook them until the pork is browned, seven to eight minutes, breaking it up with a wooden spoon as it cooks.

Stir in the cornstarch, tomato paste and sugar. Add the crushed tomatoes and chicken broth. Bring the mixture to a low boil, then reduce the heat to low.

Stir in the cream, Parmesan and ricotta until the sauce is smooth. Stir in the cooked pasta. Top servings of pasta with Asiago.

Store lasagna soup in an airtight container in the refrigerator. Enjoy it within four days.

If you’re anticipating leftovers when you’re preparing the recipe, store the pasta separately from the soup and add it to individual servings when reheating it to preserve the pasta’s texture and the soup’s consistency.

Reheat lasagna soup in a saucepan over medium-low heat on the stovetop or in a vented, microwave-safe container until it’s hot. If you stored the soup with the pasta mixed in, you may want to add a splash of chicken broth, as the pasta will have soaked up some of the broth as it sat in the refrigerator.

Yes, you can freeze Panera lasagna soup for up to three months in an airtight, freezer-safe container. Thaw the frozen soup overnight in the refrigerator before reheating it.

If you want to avoid dirtying a pot, you can cook the pasta directly in the soup. Add the uncooked pasta to the pot after adding the broth and tomatoes, and after bringing the soup to a boil. If you do, increase the amount of broth and crushed tomatoes, as the pasta will absorb moisture as it cooks. Add an additional 15 ounces of crushed tomatoes and 1 to 2 cups of chicken broth.

Keep in mind that pasta mixed into the soup will become mushy if stored as leftovers in the refrigerator.

For a thicker lasagna soup, increase the cornstarch by 1 to 2 tablespoons or reduce the chicken broth in the recipe.

To replicate the creamy, rich texture and cheesy flavor of Panera’s lasagna soup, you’ll need a trio of Parmesan, Asiago and ricotta cheeses. Choose whole-milk ricotta for maximum richness, and grate the Parm and Asiago fresh from a block for the best flavor and smoothest texture.

#5768. Romans may have set fire to Celtic town during conquest to 'humiliate' the enemy, archaeologists find

Archaeologists discovered a first-century-B.C. military camp that was erected during Emperor Augustus' Cantabrian Wars.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Archaeologists have unearthed a Roman military camp in Spain, where they found fragments of weapons and metal tacks from soldiers' shoes. The camp was used during the Cantabrian Wars fought by Emperor Augustus as he expanded the Roman Empire in the first century B.C.

The fortified military camp is made up of an earthen platform roughly 39 feet (12 meters) wide, which is surrounded by a stone perimeter wall about 5 feet (1.5 m) thick, according to a translated statement. Uncovered during excavations earlier this year, the camp is situated about one-third of a mile (500 m) from where an Iron Age settlement was once inhabited by the Cantabri people, a group of Celtic tribes that lived among the Cantabrian Mountains in modern-day northern Spain.

The Cantabri fiercely resisted Roman rule for a decade, but in 19 B.C., they were conquered by the military commanded by Emperor Augustus. The Cantabrian Wars marked the end of local control over the territory, as the Iberian Peninsula then came under control of the Roman Empire.

Archaeologists work at the site of the Roman fort.

"It's not easy to find a camp associated with a hill fort in this condition," Eduardo González Gómez de Agüero, an archaeologist at the University of León who co-directed the excavation project, said in the statement.

Among the well-preserved remains at the camp were metal weapon fragments and small iron tacks known as hobnails, which were hammered into the bottom of Roman soldiers' leather military sandals to help provide traction on rocky and muddy terrain. These objects are evidence that the Roman military was prepared to attack and subdue the Iron Age community, according to the statement.

The archaeologists also uncovered evidence of a major fire that destroyed most of the pre-Roman settlement, including two temples. González Gómez de Agüero suggested that the Romans may have deliberately set the fire as part of their conquest.

"It's not just about defeating the enemy, but about humiliating them, erasing their identity, and breaking their connection to the land," he said in the statement.

Researchers are still working on establishing the chronology of the Romans' final attack on the Cantabri and want to understand if the settlement was destroyed in a single event or over time. Previously, another research team found 66 Roman camps in northern Spain, showing the immensity of the empire's attacks against the Cantabri.

The latest find is helping researchers understand the scale and location of Rome's fight for control of the Iberian Peninsula.

"It is an important step in understanding how the conquest was carried out on the Cantabrian slope of León, an area where it was necessary to link the Roman camps with Iron Age settlements," Carlos Fernández Rodríguez, an archaeologist at the University of León, said in the statement.

Can you identify these historical objects of war? Test your smarts with our weapons of the world quiz!

Kristina Killgrove is a staff writer at Live Science with a focus on archaeology and paleoanthropology news. Her articles have also appeared in venues such as Forbes, Smithsonian, and Mental Floss. Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

#5767. Tesla remote operators keep crashing its ‘Robotaxis,’ data shows

Tesla’s latest crash reports to federal regulators add two more “Robotaxi” crashes where a remote operator, not the car’s autonomous system, was driving when it hit something.

That brings it to five in the data now. And every remote takeover that has shown up in a Tesla crash report has ended in a collision.

Both crashes happened in August and landed in NHTSA’s September release of Standing General Order data. Tesla now has 36 unique “Robotaxi” incident reports on file, up from 22 when we last looked at this in July.

In Dallas (report 13781-16678), a Model Y was stopped on a residential street, wedged between a concrete mixer truck on the right and a parked SUV on the left. “The remote support operator remotely took control and while moving forward, the vehicle made contact with the SUV at approximately 0.40MPH,” Tesla wrote. No passengers were aboard.

In Austin (report 13781-16679), a remote operator “was in control” of a Model Y going straight through an alley, with a utility pole on one side and a dumpster on the other. It hit the dumpster at about 1 mph. Again, no passengers.

Both are property-damage-only, both are at walking speed, and both carry Tesla’s structured code for Driver/Operator Type: “Remote (Commercial / Test).” That’s the same field Tesla first used on the Houston tree-stump crash in May. Three of the five are now formally coded as remote operation.

The other two show up only because Tesla unredacted its crash narratives in May. In July 2025 in Austin, a safety monitor couldn’t get the car to proceed, a teleoperator took over, accelerated, turned left, and drove it up a curb into a metal fence at 8 mph. In January 2026, again in Austin, a teleoperator took over for navigation help and drove into a construction barricade at 9 mph.

Line them up and it’s one script. The autonomous system gets stuck or needs help, a human takes the car over remotely, and the human crashes it.

Here’s the problem. Tesla doesn’t disclose how often remote operators intervene, so there’s no way to calculate a rate. But five remote takeovers have surfaced in the crash data, and all five ended in contact. Five for five.

Tesla’s main robotaxi competitor handles this differently, and it said so on the record. In a February 2026 letter to Senator Ed Markey, Waymo wrote that it “has not used remote driving or ‘tele-operations’ where a human performs the Dynamic Driving Task.” Its remote staff give the Waymo Driver guidance it can accept or reject. They don’t steer.

Waymo has one narrow exception, and it describes almost exactly these Tesla scenarios: a trained agent can nudge a stuck vehicle forward at 2 mph at fixed steering angles to clear a lane. Waymo says that tool has never been used outside of training.

So both companies built a way to rescue a stuck car. Waymo capped its version at a crawl with fixed steering and, by its own account, has never deployed it on a public road.

Color me unsurprised. Remote teleoperation includes inherent, unavoidable latency, and the available sensors limit the teleoperator's situational awareness. Together, they make teleoperation riskier than the alternative--which would be giving the rider a control stick, or Waymo's soluton of supplemental guidance to the onboard system. This is why other companies don't do teleoperation.

We flagged this pattern in July with the Houston crash, and the data has added two more since. The severity still isn’t the story. Tesla’s robotaxis are already limited to surface streets at lower speeds, and it looks like speed is even more limited when remote operators take over.

Nobody’s been hurt, and we’re talking about bumpers, a dumpster, and a tree stump at walking speed.

What it shows is how often these cars still need a person to bail them out, and how badly that goes when one does. Teleoperation is the quiet part of the autonomy stack that nobody wants to talk about. Driving a car over a cellular or satellite link means latency, a limited view, and no physical feel for the vehicle.

If you own an EV, or a robotaxi ends up in your driveway someday, powering it with home solar is one of the cheapest ways to cut your charging costs to almost nothing. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #5766. The History of This Must-Try Cheese Goes Back Nearly 1,000 Years

An authentic wheel of Grana Padano carries a signature brand burned into its rind—so you know that if you see a diamond pattern and the cheese’s name, it must have been produced in the Valle Padana (Po River Valley) of Northern Italy according to strict rules with roots in a 12th-century Cistercian monastery. At the time, the region surrounding the city of Milan was primarily soggy marshland. The monks who built the monastery—Chiaravalle Abbey—were followers of the Rule of Saint Benedict, which, along with devotion to prayer, mandated that they practice agriculture with a focus on responsible, sustainable use of land. They first figured out how to turn the marshy landscape into arable fields so they could grow high-quality feed for dairy cattle. Since milk (and fresh cheese) didn’t last long, the next challenge for the monks was how to preserve this nutritious, valuable food source to nourish themselves and their community.

The method they developed—heating the milk in copper cauldrons, adding rennet (a coagulating enzyme), cutting the curds into tiny pieces, and molding, salting, and aging the cheese—remains largely unchanged today. By producing the first iteration of what is now known around the world as Grana Padano, the monks of Chiaravalle Abbey earned the distinction of operating the world’s first cheese dairy.

Easy to like, portable, and a good source of protein, the cheese became an emblematic food, enjoyed by Italians at all levels of society through the Renaissance and into the modern era. Its iconic name, meanwhile, developed over time. The monks called it “caseus vetus”—Latin for “old cheese”—but since the peasants didn’t know Latin, they referred to it as “grana” for its distinctive grainy texture. As production spread well beyond the monastery, each province had its own grana: Cheese made in Milan was “grana Milanese,” and “grana Mantovano” was from Mantua.

It wasn’t until 1954 that the Italian government codified the name Grana Padano to unite the different varieties of grana cheese under a single wider geographical indication and that the Grana Padano Protection Consortium was established. In 1996, Grana Padano was awarded the European Union’s Protected Designation of Origin (PDO) designation. Today the consortium incorporates 134 member dairies, who together produced over 6 million wheels of cheese in 2025. In addition to safeguarding the name, the organization is also responsible for the global promotion of Grana Padano PDO and governs every detail of production to ensure that each wheel meets the strict standards established under the PDO.

The specifications start with the cows’ diet: All fresh forage and at least 75 percent of dry fodder (such as hay) must be grown within the cheese production area. Raw milk must be collected within 24 hours of milking and be partially skimmed, allowing for natural separation of the cream. After a natural whey starter is introduced to the copper (or copper-lined) milk vat, calves’ rennet is added for coagulation. The cheese is then broken into rice-size curds using a spino, which resembles a large whisk, and packed into round molds, where it sits for at least 36 hours. The wheels are immersed in brine for anywhere from 14 days to a month and aged for at least nine months and up to 24 months or longer. Wheels aged over 20 months can be labeled Riserva.

Bite into a chunk of Grana Padano PDO, and you taste the sweetness of fresh milk coupled with a savory hint of toasted nuts. Riservas may be flakier and more crystalline in texture than younger versions of the cheese but never dry or crumbly, and they must still have a delicate flavor. Grana Padano PDO is an exceptionally versatile cheese, equally delicious on a cheese board, wrapped with a slice of Prosciutto di Parma PDO and drizzled with aged balsamic for a sophisticated appetizer, as a filling for ravioli, or grated over soup or pasta.

It’s good for you, too. Lactose- and gluten-free, Grana Padano PDO is low in fat and an excellent source of protein, calcium, and vitamin B12. A 30-gram piece (just over one ounce) has as much nutrition as a pint of milk, making it a beneficial as well as tasty solution to the “afternoon slump” for both kids and adults. Whoever you share Grana Padano PDO with, be sure to tell them what they have in common with Italians from more than 1,000 years ago—that they’re savoring a taste of European culinary history.

Head to DistinctlyDeliciouslyEuropean.eu to learn more about Prosciutto di Parma PDO and Grana Padano PDO.
