#5765. As a Food Editor, 'Food Is Fuel' Never Resonated. Here's What Changed My Mind

Editor’s note: The following story references disordered eating.

“Food is fuel.” I’ve heard the cliché my entire life, and it never resonated. Food has always represented much more to me: a reason to gather, a way to celebrate holidays, a source of nostalgia and novelty. To see food as proverbial gas in the tank seemed clinical.

But food has also been a source of deep personal anxiety, and in moments, downright pain. For many years, I was obsessive and compulsive with my eating behavior. A binge-and-restrict cycle became my norm, with food acting as the currency for doling out punishment and rewards.

As I worked with a therapist and mended my relationship with food, I started seeing it as nourishment for my spirit. This was actually fairly easy—I’ve always loved to eat. But it was finding the resolve to intentionally fuel my body, especially as I’ve established a deeper practice of strength training and running, that remained a struggle.

So used to underfueling, I assumed everyone was so tired after a jog or lift that they also literally fell asleep an hour later. Not the case! Many people with robust movement routines eat enough carbs, protein, and fat—not just to achieve a baseline level of physical nourishment, but a quantity necessary to build stamina for heavier sets, increasing mileage or speed, what have you.

This kind of nutrition was new to me. It was more than salad and a plank of salmon. As I educated myself on truly energy-enhancing and recovery-supporting foods, I found ingredients I’d been conditioned to see as “unhealthy,” like bread and cheese, or white potatoes and red meat, actually offered incredible nourishment.

Throughout my teens and early adulthood, I approached health with an all-or-nothing mentality. When I learned that red meat was higher in saturated fat than legumes, I limited my intake of the former. After reading that too many carbohydrates could spike my blood sugar, potentially leading to more hunger and weight gain, I swapped my morning oatmeal for yogurt. Even though I had no medical predispositions to warrant slashing these foods, I saw the ingredients as barriers to optimal health.

At the time, my definition of “healthy” was misguidedly synonymous with “being thin.” I logged hours of endless, directionless cardio, the only intention for which was to burn the minimal calories I consumed the rest of the day.

After a brief respite in the 2010s when body-positivity and neutrality were in vogue, the pendulum has swung back with a vengeance. Today, I have the opportunity to buy a get-skinny-quick scheme in the form of certain peptide therapies, which can result in fat loss and decreased appetite. Myriad celebrities who once touted body-positivity are taking and becoming advocates for the FDA-approved versions of these weight-loss drugs, often known as GLP-1 drugs—choices that are, of course, theirs, and none of my business, but ones that are public and influential.

This is not to bash properly taking a prescribed medication: Many users report that, since taking a GLP-1 drug, they are able to comfortably exercise, or found it easier to avoid alcohol and stay sober, or even manage a binge-eating disorder. My main concern with these medications is how easy it is to access and abuse them. I’ve been in several social settings in the past months where someone has mentioned they are “microdosing” a GLP-1 drug. These are folks who do not medically need the prescription, but take the shots or pills purely to lose that “last” 5 or 10 pounds. And where things get especially sticky for me is when these conversations shift to food noise. And they almost always do.

Though there’s not a formal definition, the concept describes when thoughts of food become so intrusive they disrupt the regular flow of the day. Yet I fear many people, especially those who have a history of disordered eating, conflate food noise with any thoughts of food, even as a result of hunger—something your body should feel throughout the day, especially if it’s expending energy. When someone praises a drug for helping them forget about food altogether, something is wrong. And blaming the medication alone is a Band-Aid on a gushing wound. What we should really be focusing on is why people are ashamed to fuel themselves.

This has become something of a mantra for me lately: It is human to feel hungry, and what a privilege it is to be able to satiate myself. Look at what I can accomplish when I am truly nourished.

My strongest, most capable body might not be its most lean version. What’s more, I don’t want to rid myself of thoughts of food at the expense of losing my appetite. I love food. And I want to learn why certain ingredients, even those I’ve been conditioned to fear in the past, are beneficial to my body.

This doesn’t mean I’m switching my protein sources to exclusively chicken breast or beef because the gym rats swear by it. In fact, I probably only cook meat once or twice a week. Seafood and plant-based proteins are incredibly nutritious, each offering plenty of macronutrients—not that I’m tracking anything religiously.

If there aren’t personal dispositions from an ethical or medical perspective, there is no reason to completely omit any ingredient from one’s diet. A russet potato filled with ground beef appears in this column alongside legumes and grains, chicken breast, and sardines precisely to show that a healthy diet is a varied one, especially for active people.

Do I eat more on particularly lively days, when I can really reap the benefits of the extra energy? You bet. Do I force myself to curb my appetite on lower-activity days because I haven’t “earned” my sustenance? Not anymore. In fact, intentionally underfueling can slow metabolism as the body attempts to store nutrients. Diet culture may have turned calories into something to fear or avoid, when in reality they are simply units of measurement for energy. And we all need energy!

Sometimes it’s as simple as that. Reorienting my perspective to see food not as a reward, but a power source, changed everything. And fueling both my body and brain are equally worthy. I need complex nourishment after finishing a 10K just as much as I will after writing this essay. And it won’t just be a collection of the appropriate dose of macros to keep me going. It will be salty (or sweet) and rich, crunchy and colorful, something I genuinely crave.

#5762. Copycat Little Caesars Cheesy Bread

Skip the trip and bake a sheet pan of copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread—it's enough to serve a crowd.

Little Caesars understands that people will happily make a detour on the way home for a box of soft, garlicky bread smothered in melted cheese. I’ve been one of those people more times than I’d like to admit. This copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread recreates the chain’s Italian cheese bread (not to be confused with its equally popular Crazy Bread) with store-bought pizza dough, garlic butter and a thick layer of cheese. When it’s fresh from the oven, the bronzed, cheesy top bubbles at the edges. You’re basically guaranteed to get Instagram-worthy cheese pulls.

The most common problem I see with homemade bread recipes like this one is a gummy, underbaked center. When butter and cheese go onto raw dough, the fat and moisture sink in, and the middle never really bakes all the way through. This recipe fixes that with a quick parbake, which sets the crust before any toppings go on. It’s the same trick I use for sheet-pan pizza, and it guarantees a golden bottom with a little crunch. A single pan yields 20 pieces, which makes this a no-brainer for game day, kids sleepovers, and salad dinners that need a warm, shareable side.

Preheat the oven to 375°F. Press the pizza dough into a greased 15x10x1-inch baking pan. Cover it and let it rise in a warm place until it’s doubled in size, about 30 minutes.

Editor’s Tip: If the dough keeps springing back from the corners, cover it and let it rest for five minutes. The gluten will relax and the dough will stretch much more easily.

Prick the dough all over with a fork. Bake it until it’s lightly browned, 10 to 12 minutes.

In a small bowl, combine the melted butter and garlic. Brush the mixture over the warm dough.

Sprinkle the dough with the mozzarella, Parmesan and Italian seasoning.

Bake the bread for 20 minutes or until it’s golden brown. Cut it into 1-inch rectangles. Serve it hot with pizza sauce, if desired.

Let leftover cheesy bread cool completely, then transfer the pieces to an airtight container and refrigerate them. Don’t let it sit out for more than two hours, since the cheese topping makes it perishable. A sheet of parchment paper between layers will keep the pieces from sticking together.

Copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread lasts three to four days in the refrigerator when stored in an airtight container.

You can get a head start on copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread by parbaking the crust (following the recipe through Step 2) up to a day ahead. Let the crust cool, wrap it so it’s airtight and keep it at room temperature. When you’re ready, warm the crust in the oven for a few minutes, then brush on the garlic butter, add the toppings and bake it as directed.

Yes, copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread freezes well for up to three months. Wrap cooled pieces individually in airtight packaging, then store them together in a freezer container. Reheat the pieces straight from the freezer on a baking sheet in a 375º oven until they’re hot all the way through and the cheese has melted again.

Little Caesars keeps its exact seasoning blend a secret, but the chain describes its Italian cheese bread as mozzarella and Muenster topped with garlic, butter, Parmesan and its own Italian spices. For copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread, a store-bought Italian seasoning with dried oregano, basil, thyme and rosemary gets you very close. Add a pinch of garlic powder to the blend if you want an even stronger garlic hit.

Yes, homemade pizza dough works in this recipe for copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread. Use about 1 pound of dough so it fills the 15x10x1-inch pan to the same thickness, and let it rise until it’s doubled in size before you parbake it. Dough made with a little olive oil will bake up slightly crisper on the bottom.

Low-moisture shredded mozzarella and grated Parmesan are the best cheeses for copycat Little Caesars cheesy bread because they melt well and brown without turning greasy. Little Caesars pairs mozzarella with Muenster on its restaurant version, so swapping shredded Muenster for part of the mozzarella brings the copycat even closer to the original. Skip fresh mozzarella, which releases too much water and leaves the crust soggy.

#5760. Tiny implants can use the body itself to talk to each other

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

The network could let tiny implants sense symptoms and trigger treatments

An implant, small enough to inject through a syringe, can send electrical signals to a wearable device through the body’s own tissue

Candler Hobbs, Georgia Institute of Technology

This is a human-written story voiced by AI. Got feedback? Take our survey . (See our AI policy here .)

Medical implants are getting smaller and smarter. But getting them to talk to each other remains tricky.

Now, researchers have developed a way for tiny implantable devices to send electrical signals through the body’s own tissues. The approach could offer a low-power way for implants to work together, with one detecting a symptom and another delivering treatment, the team reports September 24 in Science.

Currently, getting devices to communicate through tissue comes with trade-offs. Some medical devices, such as insulin pumps linked to glucose monitors, use Bluetooth, which relies on radio signals. Receiving those signals requires power even when a device is only listening, increasing the need for battery power and making devices harder shrink. Radio signals also weaken as they travel through body tissue, limiting how far apart communicating devices can be.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

The new network called SWANS, the Smart Wireless Autonomous Networking System, works differently. A wearable external hub or implant generates an electric pulse that produces a small current in the surrounding tissue. Tiny implants can detect those electrical signals while using almost no power when they are only listening. And without large batteries, those implants can be small enough to inject through a syringe.

To demonstrate how the network works, the team attached motion sensors to the front legs of rats. When the animals moved their front legs, the system’s external hub sent signals through their bodies to neurostimulator implants in the sciatic nerves, causing muscles in the back legs to contract.

The synchronized limbs weren’t really the point. The experiment was designed to show that signals could travel through the body, linking different kinds of devices, says Alex Abramson, a biomedical engineer at Georgia Tech in Atlanta. It was “a proof of concept showing that you can really connect this system to anything.”

Abramson says a network of smart implants could automatically deliver a drug or stimulate a neuron exactly when needed to treat complex conditions like Parkinson’s disease. Simple signals can be sent from one implant to another, while an external hub can coordinate more complex networks of sensors and devices, possibly using artificial intelligence.

Before the new network can be used in humans, researchers will need to show that the electrical signals do not cause unintended problems like heart rhythm disruptions. Such issues  have not turned up in the experiments in rats yet, but the technology will need to be tested over longer periods of time and in larger animals, Abramson says.

Materials engineer Amir Alavi says the network’s potential could grow as other technologies improve, including wearables, AI systems and miniature, battery-free implantable sensors like those his lab is developing. “There are now several really interesting directions converging,” says Alavi, of at the University of Pittsburgh’s Swanson School of Engineering. “If those pieces eventually come together, you could have implants that are much smaller, simpler, longer-lived, and more connected than what we have today.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

J. Luo et al. Wireless electronic-free mechanical metamaterial implants. Materials Today, 2025. doi: 10.1016/j.mattod.2024.12.018

#5759. Using AI to offer personalized health advice

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Emma Pierson is at the University of California, Berkeley, Berkeley, California 94703, USA.

Although people report increasing trepidation about the effects of artificial intelligence across many domains, health care emerges as a rare area of some optimism1. AI tools, in the form of large language models (LLMs), such as the chatbot ChatGPT, are widely used for health advice. It is estimated that one-third of US adults turn to chatbots for answers to health questions2, and globally it has been suggested that about 230 million people ask ChatGPT health and wellness questions every week3. Writing in Nature, Heydari et al.4 present their response to this enormous demand by creating and assessing an AI personal health ‘agent’, which is designed to analyse an individual’s health data and offer advice.

#5758. 'Cute demo, but totally impractical': Experts are skeptical that Tesla's 'Rocket' Roadster could actually work in the real world

Tesla has unveiled a new version of its Roadster EV that can briefly take to the air using SpaceX tech. Whether it actually qualifies as a flying car is another matter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tesla has unveiled a new "flying" version of its Roadster electric vehicle (EV) that uses SpaceX tech to pull off seemingly impressive airborne acrobatics.

During a filmed demonstration at the SpaceX rocket testing facility in Texas Thursday (Oct. 15), the second-generation Tesla Roadster was shown accelerating up a curved ramp before launching itself into the air, performing a 180-degree barrel roll and touching back down to Earth.

In another stunt, the vehicle leapt over a row of parked Teslas. For safety reasons, the vehicle was operated remotely in both instances.

"Turns out, the future does have flying cars," Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk wrote on X following the demonstration.

The Roadster's airborne capabilities are made possible by a system of cold-gas thrusters developed using technology from Musk's aerospace company, SpaceX. These thrusters release bursts of high-pressure gas to generate thrust, allowing the vehicle to accelerate rapidly and, according to Tesla, become airborne for short periods.

The new Roadster will come in three variants: Performance, Thrust and Rocket. Only the Rocket is capable of flight. The Performance model is a two-seater equipped with a suction system that's designed to improve traction and handling (or drive up inverted ramps, as in Tesla's demo), while the Thrust model adds rear-mounted thrusters that Tesla claims can propel the vehicle from 0 to 60 mph (97 km/h) in under a second.

The highest-end model, Rocket, features SpaceX cold-gas thrusters positioned around the vehicle to enable short bursts of flight and in-air control. This single-seat model will be available by invitation only, according to Tesla.

On the company's website, Tesla representatives explicitly stated that the Rocket isn't approved for use on public roads and that availability depends on regional regulations. During the reveal, Musk told the audience that the Rocket "is not necessarily a safety-optimized car."

Tesla hasn't disclosed how far or for how long its EV can remain airborne, nor is it the first company to develop a hybrid road-air vehicle. There is also skepticism from outside viewers on whether the demonstration could be replicated outside of controlled scenarios.

Artificial intelligence (AI) scientist Yann LeCun ‪— a professor of data science, computer science, neural science and electrical engineering at New York University ‪— described the demonstration on X as a "marketing stunt" and questioned whether the vehicle would work in practice.

"Even assuming that 50% of the mass of the car is the compressed air, that gives you less than 10s of useful thrust (with thrust-to-weight ratio larger than 1.0). Probably more like 5 seconds, actually," he posted on the social media platform. "So the car must be an empty, lightweight shell containing big compressed air tanks. Cute demo, but totally impractical. But of course, you knew that."

He likened it to SpaceShipOne, which debuted in 2004 as the first privately funded spacecraft and made three record-setting flights before being retired.

California-based Alef Aeronautics, which is developing its own flying EVs, marked Tesla's unveiling by releasing footage of its vehicle flying over an earlier Roadster model. Alef representatives said the demonstration marked its 1,000th logged flight.

"Welcome Tesla to the flying car race, we've been here alone for eleven years," Alef CEO Jim Dukhovny said in a statement emailed to Live Science. "Having competition is a good thing, especially from such a major company as Tesla."

Unlike Tesla's compressed-gas tech, Alef's vehicle uses electric propellers for vertical takeoff and landing, with a rotating cabin and body designed to support forward flight.

Production of the new Roadster is scheduled for 2028, but Tesla doesn't have the best track record of meeting its deadlines. The company has delayed production of its Optimus humanoid robot a few times since first unveiling the machine in 2021, for example, with an initial start in 2023, before being pushed back. Only in recent months has production begun to scale to a few hundred units per week.

#5757. 8 People Hospitalized In E. Coli Outbreak Linked To Raw Milk Cheese—Here’s What To Know

Several cases have resulted in life-threatening kidney failure.

A multistate E. coli outbreak linked to a brand of raw milk cheese has hospitalized eight people so far. Here’s what you should know.

The California-based dairy brand Graziers has recalled all of its raw milk cheeses. The impacted products include Monterey Jack, Jalapeño Jack, Medium Cheddar, and Sharp Cheddar in sizes ranging from eight-ounce squares to 40-pound blocks.

Its parent company, Sierra Nevada Cheese Company, voluntarily initiated the recall on September 24 after the FDA contacted the company about its ongoing outbreak investigation and recommended a recall. The brand continues to cooperate with the investigation.

Because the FDA and CDC have not yet identified the specific lot number, production date, or source of the E. coli, Graziers has pulled all of its raw milk cheese from shelves out of an abundance of caution.

One recalled product, a 40-pound block of cheddar sold to foodservice providers, was distributed across 13 states and totaled 1,373 pounds. The broader recall, though, includes products distributed nationwide through retailers, distributors, direct customers, and online.

As of September 25, federal agencies say the outbreak spans nine states: California, Nevada, Oregon, Utah, Colorado, Michigan, Kentucky, Tennessee, and Georgia. Of the 13 cases tied to the outbreak, eight resulted in hospitalizations. Three people also developed hemolytic uremic syndrome, a serious form of kidney failure. More than half of those sickened are children age five or younger. So far, no deaths have been associated with the outbreak.

If you happen to have any Graziers raw milk cheese in your fridge, the FDA advises disposing of it or returning it to the place of purchase for a refund. The products have a long shelf life, so the agency recommends checking both refrigerators and freezers. To avoid potential cross-contamination, it also recommends cleaning and sanitizing any surfaces or containers the cheese may have touched.

If you have consumed this brand of cheese, watch for symptoms. E. coli infections can cause severe stomach cramps, diarrhea, fever, nausea, and vomiting. Symptoms can begin up to nine days after consuming contaminated food. If you experience any symptoms consistent with an E. coli infection, contact your healthcare provider immediately.

#5756. Apple Juice Bread

A simple swap from water to apple juice transforms this basic bread recipe into a subtly sweet loaf that is perfect to tear into at breakfast time with a smear of good butter on top, but it also takes a PB&J to the next level. The subtle, but unmistakable, undercurrent of apple sweetness struck me as a bit of a magic trick in a bread that, otherwise, to the eye and hand was just a soft white loaf.

I first encountered the idea at the small four-seat teppanyaki restaurant in the back of the Suiran Hotel in Kyoto, Japan, where chef Tsubasa Yamaguchi served it before my first course; it was a perfect way to start the meal. When I asked him what he did differently to prepare it, he said he just took the same rustic bread recipe he baked elsewhere and used apple juice in place of water. I was incredulous. That was it? The same amount? The same technique? Yes and yes, he said. I was hooked. It turns out the water-to-juice switcheroo gives an added taste of fall without taking anything away from the shape and texture. It’s become an instant favorite at my house both breakfast and lunch, and I expect it will be at yours.

Take care when you’re lowering the bread into the Dutch oven; it is a slightly nerve-wracking moment but a little attention is all that’s needed. You can do it!

More apple recipes here: 37 Apple Desserts That Make Us Want to Pick the Whole Orchard

Mix 4 cups (500 g) all-purpose flour, 1¾ cups apple juice, 1 Tbsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 1 Tbsp. sugar, and one ¼-oz. envelope instant yeast (about 2¼ tsp.) in a large bowl just until flour is hydrated and a very shaggy dough forms, about 1 minute. Cover bowl with plastic wrap and let dough sit at room temperature 15 minutes.

Set a small bowl of room-temperature water on work surface for easy reaching. Uncover dough, dip your hand in water and stretch one side of dough up and over to meet opposite side; turn bowl 90°. Repeat 3 more times, turning bowl 90° after each fold. (This constitutes 1 full fold.) If dough doesn’t always stretch to meet opposite side, just aim for the middle. Wet your hands and shake off excess water any time dough begins to feel sticky. Cover dough and let sit 30 minutes (set a timer). Repeat folding process to make 1 full fold. Re-cover and let dough sit another 30 minutes. Repeat folding process and resting one more time. By the last fold, dough should be significantly smoother and tighter.

Transfer dough to a generously floured surface, dust with flour, and shape into a tight ball. Dust dough with more flour. Dust a kitchen towel with flour and place inside a high-sided bowl to line. Arrange dough, seam side up, in bowl on top of towel. Tightly cover bowl with plastic wrap and let dough sit in a warm spot until puffy, about 45 minutes.

Place a rack in middle of oven, then set a large ovenproof Dutch oven with a lid on it; preheat oven to 400°. Place a 12" square of parchment paper on a flat surface. Cut a 2"-long slit in each corner toward the center. Very carefully, tip dough out onto center of parchment, trying your best not to deflate it or ruin its shape. (It’s easiest to place parchment on a flat plate so you can easily turn dough out of bowl.) Working very quickly and using a pair of kitchen shears lightly coated in vegetable oil, cut an X in center of bread. Remove pot from oven and very carefully lower dough into pot. Cover pot and return to oven.

Bake bread 35 minutes. Uncover pot, reduce oven temperature to 375°, and continue to bake bread until crust is deeply browned, 35–40 minutes more. Carefully tip loaf out onto a wire rack and let cool at least 1 hour before slicing.

Do Ahead: Bread can be baked 3 days ahead. Let cool completely; store airtight at room temperature.

#5755. 12 Dishes and Drinks Everyone Should Try in Mexico City

Mexico City is a colossal, beautifully overwhelming metropolis and an absolute paradise for those who live to eat. Across thousands of restaurants, markets, street stalls, bakeries and fondas, food from every corner of the country converges. So deciding where (and what) to eat can feel daunting.

I’ve spent over a decade writing about Mexico’s food scene, along with popping up on TV shows to talk about it occasionally. My favorite way to navigate it all is still right here on the ground with my food tours. Sidestepping the obvious spots and encouraging travelers to go deeper into the food culture here constantly reminds me of the fascinating nature of how we chilangos eat.

To me, that means having the flexibility to move between completely different culinary worlds. We can spend lunchtime standing on a crowded sidewalk, leaning over a taco drenched in salsa and lime juice, then dress up that same evening for a multicourse dinner at a dimly lit restaurant. We’re fiercely loyal to the neighborhood places that have fed us for years and know our names, but we remain insatiably curious about the next opening or dish worth crossing town for.

That same flexibility shapes the city’s food. CDMX knows exactly how to pull tradition into modernity without losing its spirit. These 12 spots offer a taste of the city’s versatility: dishes and bites deeply rooted in history but shaped by the wonderfully chaotic, ever-evolving way we eat today.

Adolfo Prieto 1364 B, Tlacoquemecatl del Valle, Benito Juárez +52 55 5559 6358

I remember being dragged out of bed early on a weekend as a teenager to have breakfast here. Although I was still drowsy, the smell of the wood-fired stoves, the café de olla (traditional “pot coffee”) bubbling away, and sounds of the older musicians playing classic tunes on their guitars were enough to bring me around. I was fully awake by the time a plate of refried beans with scrambled eggs and a stack of warm tortillas made it to the table. It’s the kind of humble combination you’d find in almost any Mexican home. At Fonda Margarita, these eggs, which are cooked over a wood fire and enriched with lard, take on a different life. Getting here early is a requirement, as they open around 6:30 a.m. and by 10 a.m., all the food is gone.

C. Chilpancingo 35, Colonia Condesa, +52 55 5264 4976

Few breakfast menus are complete without chilaquiles. What started as a way to use up day-old tortillas is now one of the country’s quintessential breakfasts: triangles of tortillas, deep-fried until crisp, then bathed in salsa. At Chilpa in leafy Condesa, several salsas are available, from a red, tomato-based version to the bright, tangy tomatillo number, all with different spice levels. Pile on your choice of cecina, shredded chicken, or fried eggs. Crema, grated cheese, and chopped onion are standard, making it easy to order a different version every visit.

If you asked my Mexican grandma what breakfast looks like, she’d say coffee and a pan dulce—more often than not that would mean a concha. The quintessential Mexican pastry makes an appearance in virtually every bakery in town, from the most traditional versions made with a combination of lard and shortening to the tender butter-forward renditions found in most modern bakeries. At Panadería Gala, a speakeasy-style bakery, the extra-bouncy conchas get an extra layer of indulgence when topped with a white or dark chocolate ganache quenelle.

The Margarita may be known worldwide as the quintessential Mexican cocktail. Legend has it that the iconic drink was first conceived in Ensenada, Baja California, and its ties to coast and borderlands are still strong. But chilangos tend to save it for specific occasions and places such as a beach vacation, a favorite cantina, or a particular restaurant where we know they’ll make a killer one. At Despacho Margarita, the classic shaken version gets a new life, alongside spicy and frozen variations. In the slushy machine there are usually two flavors: a classic tequila and lime version and a seasonal mezcal-based one that changes monthly, with unique flavors such as nopal and shiso or raspberry and cayenne pepper.

Cantinas have long been places to gather over good food and several rounds of drinks. In these laid-back establishments, both are given equal importance. At Covadonga, a cantina without background music, generations mix at the tables, from the young and cool to sixty-somethings who come to play dominoes (available on request).

Banderas have been a popular order here from the beginning: three shot glasses lined up, one with tequila, another with lime juice, and a third with sangrita, a red-hued blend of tomato and fruit juices. Together they mimic the three colors of the Mexican flag (hence the name). They’re not meant to be knocked back but rather sipped slowly, alternating between glasses.

There’s a certain type of stand you’ll see repeatedly throughout the city centered around a large, round comal, usually fired with charcoal, with a bucket of corn masa and, more often than not, two women working side by side. Gorditas, quesadillas, and sopes are all on the menu, but the tlacoyo may be the most emblematic of central Mexico and Mexico City.

Football-shaped and stuffed with your choice of fava bean purée, requesón (a soft cheese similar to ricotta), or refried beans, tlacoyos are typically topped with cactus salad for a tangy contrast, grated queso fresco, and red or green salsa, depending on your preference. They're the kind of morning or afternoon snack you might grab during errands or before wandering into the adjacent market.

Petén 248 y, Avenida Universidad, Narvarte Poniente, Benito Juárez+52 55 5536 3060

The idea of stacking meat on a rotating spit was brought to Puebla by Lebanese immigrants. Once it reached Mexico City, it evolved into one of the city’s great late-night bites: the taco al pastor. At El Vilsito, a garage by day that moonlights as one of the city’s best taquerías, they take the pastor one step further, turning it into the most Chilango version possible by making it into a torta. A warm, lightly toasted telera gets a smear of refried beans, avocado, onion, tomato, and freshly shaved pastor, all finished with melted cheese.

Calle Versalles 113, Casa Versalles, Juárez+52 55 8568 7397

You can’t talk about Mexico City without talking about its obsession with seafood. It may seem strange for a city far from the coast, but seafood from both of Mexico’s coastlines makes its way here through the second-largest seafood market in the world (surpassed only by Tokyo’s Toyosu) before being distributed across the rest of the country.

At Taller de Ostiones, clams, barnacles, and tuna arrive three times a week by air from Baja California Sur, alongside sustainably farmed oysters. Those bivalves pair perfectly with a house-made sauce that’s rich, briny, deeply umami, and slightly spicy.

Mexico City runs on fondas and street food. These casual eateries serve homestyle food that’s quick, affordable, and feels like a warm hug. Stepping inside a fonda means being a part of a daily ritual known as comida corrida, where a meal typically unfolds in four acts: a steaming bowl of soup or broth to start, followed by your choice of rice or pasta. Next comes the main course, which at some places, like Nuevo Tehuacán, is what dictates the price of the meal; meatballs in chipotle, pork with tomatillo and purslane, and chicken in peanut sauce are classics. Everything is washed down with a pitcher of freshly made agua fresca and finished with a sweet treat like a cup of gelatin. Look around, and you’ll see schoolchildren sitting elbow to elbow with office workers all savoring the comfort of one of the city’s most democratic meals.

Mostly homemade, flan is a staple of casual Mexican cooking, but it’s one of those desserts that look deceptively simple. Getting the texture just right (silky, firm, not overly sweet) is way harder than it seems, which may be why the best versions are often found in homes. Siembra may be a temple dedicated to heirloom corn masa, tacos, and antojitos, but its flan has all the right qualifications to receive just as much attention.

Fernando Montes de Oca 89, Cuauhtémoc, Colonia Condesa+52 55 5286 1258

Ice cream parlors used to be an almost daily afternoon ritual for families and couples alike. Nevería Roxy was one of the early ones, and its 1950s-diner palate of teal and red has barely changed, even in the newer locations. Alongside the classic milk- and cream-based flavors, sundaes, floats, and banana splits, you’ll find nieves.

The word literally translates to “snow,” and the closest comparison might be a sorbet, though the texture tends to be a little coarser and the flavors typically center around seasonal fruits. Mamey, an endemic fruit, may have a short season, but Roxy keeps its nieve de mamey year-round. With a faintly nutty flavor reminiscent of baked sweet potato, it’s become a local favorite. And thanks to mamey’s lush, natural creaminess, it’s easy to forget there’s no dairy in the cup.

Avenida 5 de Mayo 39, Centro Histórico de la Ciudad+52 55 5521 1787

Stepping into Dulcería de Celaya feels more like entering a 19th-century jewelry store than a candy shop. Founded in 1874, this Centro Histórico institution still retains its original art nouveau interior, complete with French mirrors, tiled floors, and hand-carved oak cabinets, all filled with traditional sweets made in-house. Among the colorful sweet treats are candied lime skins stuffed with shredded sweetened coconut. The peel is candied and tender and, unlike fresh lime rind, is not bitter, so you can go ahead and bite into the whole thing at once.

Recommendations on where to dine, shop, and stay, wherever you’re headed next.

Seek out tlacoyos, chilaquiles, and nieves in this culinary capital.

Mexico City is a colossal, beautifully overwhelming metropolis and an absolute paradise for those who live to eat. Across thousands of restaurants, markets, street stalls, bakeries and fondas, food from every corner of the country converges. So deciding where (and what) to eat can feel daunting.

I’ve spent over a decade writing about Mexico’s food scene, along with popping up on TV shows to talk about it occasionally. My favorite way to navigate it all is still right here on the ground with my food tours. Sidestepping the obvious spots and encouraging travelers to go deeper into the food culture here constantly reminds me of the fascinating nature of how we chilangos eat.

To me, that means having the flexibility to move between completely different culinary worlds. We can spend lunchtime standing on a crowded sidewalk, leaning over a taco drenched in salsa and lime juice, then dress up that same evening for a multicourse dinner at a dimly lit restaurant. #5753. The 8 Best Vacuums for Every Floor Type

I tried upright, cordless, canister, and robot vacs to find models that deliver reliable performance.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

When it comes to keeping your house clean, a reliable vacuum is a must. Vacuums are the quickest and easiest way to tackle dust bunnies, stubborn pet hair, and debris that collect in hidden corners. But not just any vacuum will do.

Finding the right vacuum is often overwhelming when you’re faced with sifting through dozens of upright models, robot vacuums, and more. A vacuum is a big investment, and an uninformed purchase often leads to disappointment if your choice turns out to be too heavy, too cumbersome, or not powerful enough. Plus, you want to pay for features you need without overspending on extras you’ll never use.

In this guide, we considered upright, cordless, canister, and robotic options from trusted brands, and evaluated them based on research and rigorous testing. We broke down key factors related to functionality, features, and maintenance to help you find the best vacuum, whether you need a pet-friendly option, one that’s powerful enough to clean up after kids, or a budget-friendly model.

More Home Appliances: Best Vacuums for Hardwood Floors • Best Dryers • Best Clothes Steamers

To find the best vacuums for every home, we first gathered a lineup of models across a range of brands, price points, and features. We deliberately chose a mix of canister, upright, stick, and robot vacuums to help ensure our selections accommodate different living situations, flooring types, and cleaning needs.

We focused on criteria like performance, ease of use, and versatility. For meaningful results, we ran each vacuum through a series of real-world tests to simulate common messes they’d be likely to come across in everyday life. We tried them on multiple surfaces like low-pile carpet, thick shag rugs, hardwood flooring, and bare concrete, seeing how well the vacuum adjusted to different demands. This also helped us assess suction power and how well the brush roll adapted to each surface type.

For debris, we chose materials that mimic typical household spills, like crumbled potato chips, popcorn, coffee, and cereal. The different weights and sizes of rubbish gave us a realistic view of how effectively the vacuum could remove the mess.

We also conducted similar tests with the handheld portions and attachments of these vacuums, using crevice tools and motorized brushes on couch cushions, on upholstery, and in tight corners. We noted how easy the tools were to maneuver, and whether they still packed enough power even when detached from the main unit.

By running each vacuum through comprehensive tests, we were able to get a clear picture of their real-world performance, so we could find dependable models for everyday cleaning.

I love using an upright vacuum for heavy-duty cleaning, and the AZ2002 packs the punch you need for tackling messes on carpets, couches, and floors. This model has two brushes: a soft roller brush and a foam-paddled brush. These work in tandem to increase contact with flooring and rugs, and I think that gives the vacuum a significant boost compared to models with just one traditional brush roll. The AZ2002 successfully picked up cat litter, dirt, and crumbs on a textured area rug.

All the cleaning modes are accessible right on the handle, so I was able to swiftly transition between carpet and laminate flooring without having to stop my cleaning session. The LED headlights brought attention to any dirt and gunk I missed during my initial swipes, too.

The AZ2002 also easily converts from an upright to a motorized canister vacuum. I especially liked using this functionality with the powered pet brush for cleaning couch cushions, so I didn’t have to haul the entire vacuum around. The hose and stick attachments extend to clean crevices and curtains, which I normally wouldn’t be able to reach.

The vacuum is on the heavier side, weighing a little over 16 pounds, but because it functions as a canister vac, I'm not too upset about sacrificing a little portability.

Full Shark AZ2002 Vertex Powered Lift-Away Upright Vacuum Review

I’ve had this vacuum from Shark for six years, and it has proved to be a reliable choice for apartments with hardwood, tile, area rugs, and two cats—so plenty of hair, litter, and dirt to go around.

I appreciate the Rocket for its lightweight build, convenient features, and usability. It transitions between hardwood and carpets using a switch right above the handle, and the base also detaches to become a handheld vacuum. The dustbin capacity is small, but that’s not uncommon for a stick vacuum. I just empty the dustbin whenever it gets too full by using the quick-release button.

The Rocket is easy to move around and underneath furniture, and it comes with useful attachments like a crevice tool, upholstery tool, and anti-allergen dusting brush for sucking up fine particles. The only bump in the road I’ve come across over the years is when I had a really shaggy carpet—the brush roll would often stop rotating and I’d have to untangle any fibers that got stuck.

You don’t get a ton of new tech with the Rocket, but I’ve found that it performs well on multiple surfaces. It also stores easily, and doesn’t require any hands-on effort emptying a bag or figuring out how to pair the appliance with a compatible app.

Full Shark HV301 Rocket Ultra-Light Corded Stick Vacuum Review

The Bissell Pet Hair Eraser has a tangle-free brush roll, and even after using the vacuum on two large area rugs and hardwood floors, I didn’t find any hair wrapped in the bristles. What I did find, however, was plenty of pet hair, dirt, and dander in the dustbin because the suction power is strong enough to agitate carpet fibers and pick up debris that might not be completely visible.

I use the Bissell Pet Hair Eraser Hand Vacuum on an everyday basis, so I was excited to see that this upright model comes with a dedicated pet hair attachment for the hose wand. I tried it on my couch cushions, and a couple of pushes back and forth removed a majority of the mess. Plus, the canister detaches from the base so you can carry it around as you clean.

The vacuum only has two settings, plush carpet and low carpet/bare floor, so it isn't as versatile as other vacs for different floor types. But I liked being able to manually turn the brush roll on and off with a control switch on the base, which helped me tackle different cleaning tasks.

As a whole, this vacuum is a great option if you have allergies or pets. The combination canister-upright design adds maneuverability, and the attachments included are helpful for more intricate cleaning.

Full Bissell Pet Hair Eraser Allergen Lift-Off Upright Vacuum Review

When I think of canister vacuums, Miele comes to mind, and I’m willing to bet I’m not the only one. The brand is known for its powerful, well-designed canister vacuums, and the Classic C1 Turbo Team is, well, a classic.

It has six suction power settings, including options for hard floors, rugs, and even furniture. The floor head has a toggle switch that you can operate with your foot, switching between cleaning carpeting and hard floors easily.

The hose is long, which I found helpful because I didn’t have to lug the entire vacuum around as I cleaned. The cord is retractable, and the hose is completely detachable, so the C1 stores easily in a closet or laundry room. There’s also a removable component that holds the crevice tool and brushes.

The suction was impressive on low-pile carpets, but we wouldn’t suggest using this model on dense, thick carpets. The floor head also often lost suction during our tests. I particularly liked using this vacuum on furniture—it glided across the seat cushions, and the attachments came in handy for cleaning in between cracks and seams.

This style of vacuum captures dirt and other messes in self-sealing dust bags, so you’ll be able to clean for longer before needing to do any maintenance.

Full Miele Classic C1 Turbo Team Bagged Canister Vacuum Review

It might seem like cleaning is my favorite thing to do, given how many vacuums I’m testing regularly, but even I need a bit of a break sometimes. The Roborock Qrevo Series QV 35A has automated the process for me, so my weekly deep cleans don’t take nearly as long.

I have an 850-square-foot apartment, and it took about 30 minutes for the vacuum to map out my entire home. It worked its way over thresholds, across varied floor types, and in tight spaces with ease. I was impressed by how well it differentiated between carpet and hard flooring, so I didn’t have to worry about my area rugs getting wet during the mopping cycle.

It took 61 minutes for the QV 35A to vacuum my entire apartment. It worked around the perimeter first, and then moved in a zigzag pattern around the rest of the interior. There are a few different power options, and I ran it on turbo for most of the cleaning cycle because I have two pets. I felt like the suction power delivered, but it did kill the battery relatively quickly.

Mopping is equally as satisfying, and it took roughly half the time to do all my laminate floors. The QV 35A automatically refills and empties its water reservoir, so I don’t really have to do anything besides turn it on and let it do its thing.

The robot vacuum attempted to work its way over the legs of my kitchen island chairs, and after a few tries, it got stuck. Obstacle avoidance was mostly accurate otherwise, but I had to manually add a no-go zone in the app so the vacuum avoids this spot in the future.

Full Roborock Qrevo Series QV 35A Robot Vacuum and Mop Review

Dyson remains a top brand when it comes to cordless stick vacuums, and I’ve personally tested several of its models. The V15 Detect vacuum comes with a laser cleaner to illuminate hidden dirt, dust, and debris on hardwood floors, and this feature has proved to be very useful for me, even in well-lit rooms. The traditional-style cleaner head works well on carpet, and neither head feels lackluster in suction power.

The cordless-to-handheld structure is nice for going from longer cleaning sessions to quick or more detailed cleaning, and I like using the hair screw tool for removing pet fur from couches, cushions, and more. Better yet, there is a built-in dusting and crevice tool that is great for quick cleanups.

An LCD screen on the handheld portion shows what size of particles you’re cleaning up in real time, along with battery levels and overall cleanliness status. The HEPA filtration is added peace of mind that you’re getting a thorough clean.

Full Dyson V15 Detect Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

If a corded stick or upright vacuum isn’t the best choice for your home cleaning needs, then check out the LVAC-300 from Levoit. It’s a cordless stick vacuum that switches between hard floors, rugs, and tiles without skipping a beat or losing suction power. According to our tester, the vacuum uses three different modes to take on an array of cleaning jobs like picking up cat litter or fine dust, without spreading material around your floors.

It only weighs a little over 6 pounds, so it’s easy to lift and maneuver, and the vacuum lays completely flat for cleaning under furniture. The LED headlight on the floor head illuminates any lingering messes in areas with less visibility.

The LVAC-300 is designed with a five-stage HEPA filter with as much efficiency as an air purifier, and it’s also washable and reusable. The dust container releases with the touch of a button, and it empties just as easily.

We’re keen on the LVAC-300’s handheld vacuum capability and the various attachments it comes with, like a mini motorized brush for couch cushions and beds, and a crevice tool that is slender enough for car seats. A minor inconvenience is that there’s nowhere to store these accessories, which might occasionally interrupt your cleaning session if you have to change tools.

Full Levoit LVAC-300 Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

We recently added the Shark PowerDetect Cordless Stick Vacuum to our cleaning tool arsenal. It looks like something from the future, but it actually solves a lot of the problems cordless stick vacuums face in the present, namely when it comes to maneuverability and small-capacity dustbins.

The flexible center pole bends at a joint, which made it much easier for our tester to get under furniture and in tight spaces around his apartment. This vacuum uses detection technology to automatically adjust suction power based on the type of flooring and size of mess, so you don’t have to change manually and potentially miss spills or hidden debris. The floor head light helped illuminate dirt better, too.

Another useful feature is the docking station. Once our tester was finished cleaning, he locked the vacuum back onto the base, and it automatically suctioned collected dirt, hair, and dust out of the main dustbin. This doesn’t entirely offset the small dustbin capacity on the vacuum itself, but it’s nice not having to manually empty the receptacle after every clean.

Full Shark PowerDetect Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

We have several roundups of vacuums, including cordless stick models and robots. This guide is dedicated to the top overall vacuums we’ve tested, but there are still a few other units worth considering depending on your cleaning needs.

Miele Guard L1: This canister vacuum offers convenience and power for cleaning different floor types. The Classic C1 Turbo Team we recommend above comes with a powered carpet brush, while the L1 does not. It does have longer cable reach and an illuminated display, but the C1 is the better, more affordable selection if you have a mix of surface types in your home.

Shark PowerDetect: This is a buy-up version of the Shark PowerDetect Speed we recommend above. It comes with the same flexible hinge for cleaning under furniture and several intuitive features, including automatic suction adjustment and a cleaner head with LED lights for illuminating dirt. With the regular PowerDetect, you get 10 more minutes of run time and one more level of detection (the vacuum can tell when it’s in a corner or up against a wall and ramp up the suction accordingly). But you get more bang for your buck with the Speed.

Many vacuums are available in both corded and cordless models. Corded vacuums offer unlimited run time, but require a nearby outlet. Cordless or battery-powered models are convenient to use, as you can just pick them up and start cleaning, but they have a limited runtime.

The best option for you depends on how you like to clean—do you vacuum your whole house in one go? If so, go for a corded model. Or do you normally clean one room at a time? Then a cordless option might work just fine for your needs.

Different vacuums are better suited for cleaning hard floors or carpeting, and some feature heads that raise or lower to meet the height of the surface you’re vacuuming. If you have a combination of flooring types, you may want to look for a vacuum with multiple heads and attachments, too.

Vacuum brush rolls, or “beater bars,” come in several different styles, and each is made for specific cleaning tasks and flooring. The most common types you’ll see are bristle, soft, and tangle-free. Bristle rolls are designed with stiff, nylon bristles that are ideal for thick carpets because they lift dirt effectively. They are less optimal on delicate surfaces as their harsh structure causes scratches or other damage.

For hardwood floors, or homes with a mix of surfaces, a vacuum with a soft roll brush is what you need. They’re gentle yet efficient, picking up fine particles without scattering. Tangle-free is best for pet owners, since they resist hair wrapping and are easier to maintain.

Some of today’s vacuums feature dual brush rolls, or have detachable vacuum heads for adjusting to different situations.

Capacity refers to how much dust, dirt, and grime the vacuum can hold before the dustbin needs emptying. Bigger capacities are better for larger homes or heavy-duty cleaning, while smaller dustbins are better for compact spaces or low-lift tasks. Upright vacuums typically have a dustbin capacity of 0.75 liters or more, while stick vacs only hold a couple of quarts at a time.

Maneuverability determines how easily the vacuum navigates around furniture, corners, and other obstacles. Features like swivel steering, lightweight builds, and sturdy wheels all contribute to better control. Typically, vacuums with bigger capacity are more difficult to maneuver around, usually due to a combination of size and weight. Striking the right balance between capacity and maneuverability will help you find a vacuum that’s both efficient and easy to handle.

The suction power of vacuums is commonly measured in Pascals (Pa), a unit that gauges pressure, but some brands—including Dyson—list airwatts, or the watts used by the vacuum to move a unit of air through its nozzle, instead. Not all vacuums have their suction power listed, but when comparing different models, keep in mind that high Pa or airwatts equates to more suction power.

Vacuums vary widely in price ranging from as little as $20 to $1,000 or more. Budget-friendly vacuums tend to have less powerful suction and fewer features, and if it’s a cordless model, its battery life will likely be shorter. Premium vacuums, on the other hand, typically have an ergonomic design that’s more comfortable to operate for longer periods, and their suction power is often noticeably better.

While it’s certainly tempting to give up vacuuming all together, a robot vacuum generally isn’t going to be sufficient to keep your home clean. These vacuums are designed for light-duty cleaning, and they simply can’t match the suction power of a full-size vacuum, especially on carpeting. You might be able to get away with using just a robot vacuum if you have a small home, no pets, and solely hard floors, but I think you’d still find yourself needing another vacuum to clean up occasional messes.

If you have pets, you know that pet hair wreaks havoc on a vacuum. A few passes over a dirty rug, and all of a sudden there’s a wad of hair wrapped around the brush roll, forcing you to stop and untangle it. To avoid problems like this, I highly recommend vacuums designed for pet hair. These models often have anti-tangle brush rolls and good filters, ensuring they capture all the dirt and dander that your furry friends bring into your home.

While often neglected, vacuum cleaner maintenance is key to keeping your machine running properly and ensuring it will last for years to come. You’ll want to make sure you’re emptying the bag or dustbin as often as recommended, as well as washing or replacing filters. Periodically cleaning the brush roll improves suction power, too.

Some vacuums are easier to maintain than others, and you’ll want to be honest with yourself about how much maintenance you’re willing to do—otherwise, your expensive tool might break sooner than expected.

Amber is a Reviews Editor with bylines on Popular Mechanics, Runner’s World, Bicycling, and Best Products sites. Specializing in kitchen gadgets, small appliances, lifestyle, and consumer tech, she brings hands-on testing and a detail-oriented approach to every review. In her free time you can likely find her trading trinkets at an EDM festival, searching for hidden gems at thrift stores, or cuddling with her two cats.

#5752. World's fastest AI-powered racing drone outflies the best human pilots

An AI-operated drone learned to fly from scratch and navigated a race course at speeds over 60 mph, using a single camera.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A drone powered by artificial intelligence (AI) has completed a championship race course faster than some of the world's best human drone pilots.

In a study published Oct. 14 in the journal Science Robotics, scientists created an AI system capable of pushing a quadcopter through a demanding course at over 60 mph (100 km/h), using little more than a single forward-facing camera, an inertial measurement unit, and a tiny onboard computer.

The system, called MonoRace, even won the 2025 Abu Dhabi Autonomous Racing League drone racing competition, outperforming every rival autonomous team and posting faster times than three former human world-champion drone pilots.

MonoRace accomplished this without the aid of GPS, external motion-capture systems, stereo cameras, or a preloaded map of the course. The system is an onboard autonomous flight model created by researchers at the Delft University of Technology's Micro Air Vehicles Lab in the Netherlands. Its name reflects its central design feature; "mono" refers to its monocular vision. The drone relies on a single rolling shutter camera and monitors its own motion with an internal measurement unit (IMU) that gauges acceleration and rotation.

The limited sensor package feeds a neural network, a machine-learning model that uses interconnected artificial neurons to learn patterns from data. This neural network identifies racing gates and estimates the drone's position and motion to generate motor commands. Then, it sends the commands to the four propellers at a rate of 500 times per second through the drone's flight controller.

The system fits a drone that weighs a mere 966 grams (2.13 pounds) and measures approximately 11.8 x 11 x 3.9 inches (30 x 28 x 10 centimeters). Because the MonoRace hardware was so compact, lightweight and computationally efficient, the team was able to operate it entirely aboard the drone.

On the Abu Dhabi course, MonoRace completed two laps around 11 gates in 16.56 seconds, hitting a top speed of approximately 63 mph (102 km/h).

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

A recently released video tracks MonoRace's development and performance in the competition, with real flight sequences demonstrating how the drone banks sharply, accelerates through gates, and navigates a course without human assistance.

However, some of the sequences are computer-generated. Study co-author Guido de Croon, a professor at the Delft University of Technology, said those are intentional visual effects. The only fully simulated imagery appears at about the two-minute mark, during the segment showing the training process.

"Here we show the gates in a visual simulator for the video but the real training happened in a stripped-down, pure physics simulator that ran really fast," de Croon told Live Science in an email. "Simulating vision was not done during training, as the control neural network receives an estimated state as input, which comes from a different perception and state estimation process."

The rest of the footage is real, although some shots include overlays showing how the drone interprets its camera feed. A convolutional neural network segments the white gates, sometimes displaying them as red in the normal camera image. Small circles mark detected gate corners, red lines indicate gate edges, and a blue overlay shows the portion of the image where the drone expects to find the next gate.

Scientists trained the MonoRace model with reinforcement learning, a machine learning method in which an agent repeatedly attempts a task and receives feedback based on its success or failure.

"The reinforcement learning ran in simulation, because it needs lots of trial and error — like millions of tries," de Croon said. "It actually learns to fly from scratch."

The network learned to translate the drone's position on the track, velocity and orientation directly into motor commands. According to de Croon, early training focused on the basics, like avoiding crashes and reaching the first gate. As the training progressed, the system learned to advance farther along the course and fly at higher speeds.

The researchers didn't train the system on a single aircraft. Instead, they varied dimensions such as weight, voltage, drag, sensor noise and propeller condition so the AI model could cope with real-world variations and imperfections.

"That ensures that the trained network can control different drones," de Croon said.

MonoRace could become a training tool for human drone pilots, "a bit like powerful chess programs have also inspired humans playing the game," de Croon said.

Its broader significance lies in demonstrating advancements in physical AI, especially how a robot can perceive, decide and act at high speeds despite limited sensing and computing power. The control network runs on a microcontroller-class chip, meaning that sophisticated control doesn't require a large computer.

De Croon noted that a similarly sized network could optimize for minimal energy use rather than minimum lap time. Because most machines rely on small processors and conventional controllers, energy-optimal neural control could eventually reduce power consumption in everyday robotics, he said.

Bahnam, S. A., Ferede, R., Blaha, T. M., Lang, A. E., Lucassen, E., Missinne, Q., Verraest, A. E. C., De Wagter, C., & De Croon, G. C. H. E. (2026). MonoRace: Robust monocular AI for champion-level drone racing. Science Robotics, 11(119). https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.aee9250

#5751. I've Maxed Out My Bookshelf, But These 10 Cookbooks Are Worth Making Space For

A recipe developer’s picks for the season’s most exciting new releases.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

Fall is prime cookbook season, and this year I’ve been especially flooded with new releases featuring everything from high-protein to baking to weeknight dinner—all competing for a spot on my (totally loaded) bookshelves. As a recipe developer who regularly reviews cookbooks, I’ve read hundreds of cookbooks over the years, and I’m not easily impressed by beautiful design or gorgeous photography—and I’m allergic to anything generic.

That said, from the never-ending list of options, these 10 cookbooks stood out to me. They range in subject from the expected (weeknight dinner, baking) to the less common (cookbook as memoir, cookbook as paranormal storyteller). But regardless of subject matter, each has a distinct voice and point of view—and that’s what makes them worth a second flip through.

Here are the 10 cookbooks I’m making shelf space for this season.

Caroline Chambers’ first cookbook delivered a full slate of approachable, innovative weeknight meals—and her second does not disappoint. From creative mashups (Ramen Vongole) to classic standbys (The Only Egg Salad You’ll Ever Need), each recipe left me excited to cook it (and, as a recipe developer, just a little jealous that I hadn’t come up with the idea first!)

Beyond the smart ideas and execution, I especially appreciated the book’s practical structure and organization: the recipes are grouped into chapters based on cook time, so you can easily find a recipe that suits your time frame, and the ingredient prep is included in the instructions, so you know you won’t get slapped with prep time in addition to the advertised cook time. A 15-minute recipe takes 15 minutes, start to finish—and isn’t that refreshing?

I can’t wait to make: Green Curry Wontons

I dabble in cocktail-making, but I LOVE understanding the how and why behind them, so Good Spirits is my idea of a good read. Here, master cocktail-maker Tiffany Barriere is sharing a comprehensive, concise overview of the world’s most classic cocktails, from how they were created to how you can whip up a killer version yourself.

Interspersed between the cocktails are sidebars that offer deeper dives into specific spirits, bar tools, and mixology—the nerd in me absolutely loved these!—along with stories highlighting significant Black bartenders and their contributions to cocktail culture over the decades. Whether you’re here for these stories or want to level up your own bartending, this book promises a good time.

Today’s cookbooks usually try to solve a problem or explore a culture, but Carla Lalli’s latest is doing neither. More a collection of personal essays than a cookbook, Food Is A Feeling pushes the boundaries of what I believed a cookbook could be—in a way that both surprised and impressed me.

Weeknight cookbooks are only successful if the author truly understands their audience—and Alex Snodgrass has clearly listened to hers. Packed with prep-ahead breakfasts, fast lunches, and pantry-staple dinners, this cookbook is tuned into the reality of everyday life, and focused on meeting the 21st-century home cook where they are.

The photographs are beautiful, and the recipes promise big, bold (approachable!) flavors, but beyond that, I especially appreciated the tiny practical design details that make this book both helpful and accessible. The dietary sidebar highlights recipes you can make ahead, and the side “bursts” with freezer tips and other kitchen info. Print space is valuable real estate, so their presence shows real dedication to making the book as useful as possible—no matter what restrictions you have.

I can’t wait to make: Indian Butter Chicken Meatballs

Carla Hall’s first baking book has been a long time coming, but I’m here to say it was totally worth the wait. Though she’s mostly known as a savory chef, Carla definitely knows her way around cake, pastries, and biscuits (in particular!), and, if anything, her savory expertise only makes her bakes more unique (Chocolate Pumpernickel Pancakes? Rustic Glazed Carrot Tart with Loaded Crumble? Sign me up.)

As someone who bakes a lot, I especially appreciated Carla’s precise (and sometimes brilliantly unorthodox) technique. She’s not afraid to tell us to temper chocolate in the microwave or take the internal temperature of our baked goods. And she’s equally eager to offer new approaches to traditional recipes (did you know you can make curd in the microwave?!) Carla respects our ability to learn and bake at her level, and through that, she instills us with the confidence to believe we actually can.

I can’t wait to make: Butternut Squash Beignets with Spiced Sugar

I’ve flipped through dozens of Mediterranean cookbooks, but Ayesha Nurdjaya’s exuberant, Mediterranean-by-way-of-Brooklyn is so compelling I couldn’t put it down. Filled with unapologetic takes on Mediterranean favorites (sweet potatoes with feta butter and chocolate, anyone?), this book is exploding with so much cooking joy, you’ll find yourself reaching for it every weeknight.

Ayesha’s restaurants emphasize communal eating, and I especially love how apparent that is in her book. There’s a whole chapter titled “My Dips Don’t Lie” featuring (you guessed it!) a full gamut of dips from beet tzatziki to “Gravesend Onion Dip.” There’s also an index at the front of the book which organizes recipes into “playlists” so you can easily find the perfect recipe for any occasion. The musical chapter titles continue throughout the book, by the way—just another detail that makes this book so fun.

I’ve been dying to eat at a cha chaan teng in Hong Kong since I developed a recipe for the iconic diner French toast, and while I still haven’t convinced my husband to brave the long plane ride, flipping through this cookbook almost transports me there. Part historical culture study, part cookbook, Cha Chaan Teng offers an immersive deep dive into this unique genre of Hong Kong restaurant, along with an array of intriguing recipes to make at home.

Reading through the recipes, I was struck by how familiar yet different many of them were. The food of the cha chaan teng is a blurred fusion of British, Chinese, Western, and working-class food, and the resulting dishes reflect that. There’s pork chops baked into rice, beef brisket in a curry sauce, and spaghetti bolognese made with cream of chicken soup. There’s also hot dogs served in milk buns with a smear of Miracle Whip—apparently quite traditional. I won’t knock it till I try it!

I can’t wait to make: Perfect Scrambled Egg Sandwich

Food is more than food: it’s cultural history and storytelling, and Amy Bruni’s Cuisine To Kill For is a powerful (and in some cases, gruesome!) reminder of just that. Filled with dozens of historical and modern recipes accompanied by the true crime stories that inspired them, this cookbook is a thrilling read—and if you dare to cook from it, it promises to be an even more thrilling eat.

Like Food Is A Feeling, Bruni lets storytelling guide the recipes. Each deeply dark (and possibly totally fantastical) story is rooted in a place said to be haunted, and the accompanying recipe for each story is either a historical food that’s been re-engineered for a modern audience, or something modern that’s somehow connected to the location the story took place. Whether or not you decide to make anything in it, you won’t be able to put it down.

I can’t wait to make: Lemon Toast (H.H. Holmes’ Murder Castle)

I love the recent Ottolenghi cookbooks for the way they push the envelope while staying totally weeknight-friendly, and this latest one takes that tricky balance to a whole new level. Filled with smart, beautiful recipes that deliver big flavor for minimal effort, Simple Too is taking ease and simplicity in the kitchen to the extreme—with brilliant results.

I can’t wait to make: Butternut “Parmigiana”

I’ve never been to Georgia, but after reading this cookbook, I want to book a flight immediately so I can eat there. Written in partnership by Georgian chef Meriko Gubedladze and American food writer Benjamin Kemper, this culinary love letter is filled with vibrant, intriguing food, paired with plenty of helpful culinary facts and history so we can better understand it.

One thing I especially love is that this cookbook doesn’t overcomplicate the food—but it also doesn’t attempt to tone it down or assimilate it into the broader American culture. The pantry section succinctly describes a few Georgian staples—dried marigold, barberries, and tkemali—and then uses them throughout the recipes. The book describes unfamiliar dishes in clear terms without unnecessarily comparing them to American dishes. And while Khachapuri makes an appearance, make no mistake: there’s so much more here than just cheesy bread.

I can’t wait to make: Hand-Cut Pasta with Caramelized Onions and Greek Yogurt
